# Gotchas — the long-form record

**This is the evidence behind the rules.** `CLAUDE.md`'s Gotchas section carries each rule, where
it lives, and what breaks if you undo it. This file carries the rest: how the defect was found,
what it emitted, the measurements with the commits they were taken at, the negative controls, and
the alternatives that were tried and refused.

Read the entry here before changing the code it describes. Two habits this file exists to support:
a **measured refusal is a result** — several entries record an approach that was built, measured
and rejected, and re-attempting one costs a session; and a **control that does not discriminate is
a test that is not testing**, which is this repo's most frequently recurring mistake.

- **Nothing may call `cs.disasm` directly. Every decode goes through `disasm/capstoneWindow.ts`.** capstone-wasm's linear memory is a fixed **16 MiB that cannot grow** (its `emscripten_resize_heap` aborts immediately), the input is copied onto a **~65.6 KiB WASM stack** by emscripten's `ccall`/`stackAlloc`, and `cs_disasm` allocates one contiguous `cs_insn[]` (~240 B/insn) for the whole window. The old `CHUNK_SIZE` of 0x10000 sat within **1 KiB** of the stack cliff: 65536-byte windows decode, 66560-byte ones throw `table index is out of bounds` and leave the module permanently dead. Going over is **silent** — `disasm` *throws* when it decodes nothing, and every scan loop read a throw as "this byte is not code, skip one", which is the right reading for an undecodable byte and exactly the wrong one for a dead engine. Measured: `hybridDisassemble` over a real `.text` grown to 0.5/1/2/4 MiB returned **12.5% / 7.2% / 4.6% / 3.2%** of its instructions, raised nothing, and afterwards could not decode a single `nop`. `createScan` clamps to `CS_WINDOW_BYTES` (0x2000) and `CS_MAX_INSNS_PER_CALL` (2048) and probes the engine after a run of failed decodes, so exhaustion surfaces as `CapstoneUnavailableError` → `analysisPhase: "failed"`. Smaller windows are also *faster*. **AND THE CEILING IS NOT WORTH LIFTING FOR SPEED — that is measured, at 0.035%.** The tempting reading of the paragraph above is that these bounds cost throughput and that rebuilding capstone.wasm with `-sALLOW_MEMORY_GROWTH` would buy it back (`peek-a-bin-ktp`). Measured at `07b1d09`, after `peek-a-bin-fdi8`: the fixed cost of a `cs_disasm` call is **0.56-0.58 µs**, so collapsing a whole section into ONE call instead of the 11 and 108 the production window uses is worth **0.01 ms of 18.5 and 0.06 ms of 169.7 — 0.035% on both images**. Note also that the per-call cost *fell* from the ~2.5 µs `peek-a-bin-iqzu` measured, because most of it was capstone-wasm's own marshalling setup, which `fdi8` removed — so that bead got **less** attractive, not more, and CLAUDE.md's own session-21 handoff claimed the opposite without measuring. The bounds exist for the silent-death reason above and must stay whatever else changes. A source-scraping drift guard in `__tests__/capstoneWindow.test.ts` fails if any file under `src/` outside `capstoneWindow.ts` contains `.disasm(`, `cs_disasm` or `loadCapstone(`.

- **…and the decoder UNDER that bound is now ours, not capstone-wasm's: `disasm/capstoneReader.ts` marshals each `cs_insn` by hand, for ~3x.** Measured at `488ddde` over eight images (48 KiB to 3817 KiB of `.text`, 15504 to 1047308 instructions), capstone-wasm's per-instruction cost is **83-86% JS marshalling and ~16% decode** — a full `Capstone.prototype.disasm` is 3.09-3.81 µs/instruction against 0.51-0.60 µs for `cs_disasm` plus every `ccall` it makes and zero per-instruction JS, and the cost is attributed **by name** as well as by subtraction (the dependency's own `Capstone.readInsn`, called directly, lands on the full figure). The single dominant component is `readStruct`'s accumulator, `fields.reduce((obj, field) => ({ ...obj, [field.name]: value }), {})`: a computed key defeats V8's fast object path and costs ~1.5-1.8 µs/instruction on its own, **roughly three times the decoder** (peek-a-bin-62zy). Since `peek-a-bin-x40u`, `peek-a-bin-iqzu` and `peek-a-bin-kis` every consumer free-rides one decode per section, so this is ~3x off the load's dominant term at a stroke (peek-a-bin-fdi8). Nine things:
  - **It is a drop-in because `CapstoneHandle` is STRUCTURAL and the emscripten Module is reachable.** `createScan`'s scan logic is untouched: it takes an `arch?` plus `disasm(code, opts): RawInsn[]` and neither knows nor cares which reader is behind it. And capstone-wasm's factory opens `var Module = moduleArg`, so an object passed to `loadCapstone(obj)` **is** the Module, populated in place by the time the promise resolves (`readyPromiseResolve(Module)`), carrying `HEAPU8`/`HEAP16`/`HEAPU32`/`getValue`/`UTF8ToString`/`ccall` — everything a hand reader needs.
  - **THE ABI IS HARD-CODED AND A VERSION BUMP CAN CHANGE IT SILENTLY.** `id` i32 @0, `address` i64 @8, `size` i16 @16, `bytes` 24 raw bytes @18, `mnemonic` 32-byte string @42, `opStr` 160-byte string @74, detail pointer i32 @236, **stride 240** — derived from capstone-wasm's `INSN_FIELDS` plus its `sizeOfStruct` padding rule and then verified against the shipped `capstone.wasm` rather than only computed. A wrong offset does not throw; it produces a plausible mnemonic and a plausible operand string for **every instruction in the tool**, and every other gate here would go on passing. The mitigation is `__tests__/capstoneReader.test.ts`.
  - **That test is the oracle, and it is negative-controlled at nine offsets.** Both readers decode the same bytes at the same address and every field is compared — address, size, mnemonic, opStr and the bytes' contents. It cannot read vacuously (it asserts non-zero counts) and `fastCapstoneHandle` takes its `CsInsnAbi` as an argument **for no other reason than the control**: each field is perturbed in turn and the comparison must then fail. Measured, every one of the nine is detected by a **difference** rather than by escaping through a throw — 1092-1911 differing rows against a 1911-instruction batch. The inputs are hand-written sequences plus deterministic pseudo-random bytes swept the way production sweeps (advance one byte on a window that decodes nothing), because a single `cs_disasm` over random bytes stops at the first bad byte and returns two instructions; swept, the same 8 KiB yields 2766/3218/742 instructions on x86-64/x86-32/A64. A long run of valid encodings is in there too, because `stride` and `address` only matter from the *second* instruction of a batch onward and the random cases put barely any load on them (2 and 3 differing rows, against 1911 with the run).
  - **A RUNTIME self-check was considered and REFUSED.** It would be a new way for startup to fail, on the decoder the entire tool is downstream of, to catch a change that only ever arrives with a `package.json` edit — which is exactly when a test runs. Keep the check in the test; do not move it into the product.
  - **It sits BELOW `createScan`, not beside it, and the drift guard was widened rather than sidestepped.** This is a *decoder*, in the position `Capstone.prototype.disasm` occupied; it does no windowing and must not, since `createScan` has already clamped the window and the count against the two measured WASM ceilings. The existing guard is textual and this reader never writes `.disasm(` — it reaches the export as `ccall("cs_disasm", …)` — so `capstoneWindow.test.ts` now also fails on **`"cs_disasm"`/`_cs_disasm` anywhere under `src/` outside `capstoneReader.ts`**, and on **`loadCapstone(` anywhere under `src/` outside it**. All three guards check the exemption in **both** directions: the exempt file must itself match, or a rename would make them read clean by matching nothing. The `cs_disasm` pattern deliberately matches a *call* (a quoted name, or emscripten's `_cs_disasm` alias) and not the word, because three modules discuss `cs_disasm` in prose; `.d.ts` files are excluded, being declarations that emit no code.
  - **The `loadCapstone` guard is the one protecting the SPEEDUP rather than correctness, and nothing else could see it.** `loadCapstone` is a singleton — the second call returns immediately and populates nothing — so a new bootstrap site calling it directly does not fail: it wins the race, leaves the Module unpopulated, and silently drops the whole tool back onto the dependency's reader at ~3x the cost per instruction. Retention is therefore **optional by construction**: `loadCapstoneModule` verifies the object actually came back populated, and `capstoneHandle` falls back to `cs` itself otherwise, so the worker's three bootstrap paths and their error race can only ever cost performance.
  - **MCP had to be threaded whatever else was.** `npm run corpus` drives `FileSession` → `mcp/disasm.ts`, not the worker, so an unthreaded MCP would leave the repo's main correctness harness verifying a decoder the app does not use. All five `corpus/` harnesses are threaded too, so `corpus:arm64`'s gated sweep-cache differential now runs through the fast reader.
  - **Two behaviour deltas, both bounded.** `bytes` is a slice of exactly `size` where capstone-wasm slices a fixed 24 and `subarray`s it — same contents, same length, same zero `byteOffset`, only `bytes.buffer.byteLength` differs, which makes it smaller over `postMessage`; **do not test that length in either direction**, per the `gridScan` gotcha. And a window that decodes nothing returns `[]` where capstone-wasm throws: `createScan`'s `run` catches the throw into `insns = undefined` and then takes the same branch as an empty list, and `capstoneDecodes` catches it into `false`, which is what an empty list yields too — so the engine-death probe is untouched and the change only removes an `Error` construction on the undecodable windows a `.text` full of padding produces by the hundred. The **address is bit-for-bit** capstone-wasm's, pathological case included: above `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER` both leave a `BigInt` in a field `RawInsn` types as `number`.
  - **Measured on this tree at `adfe1b5`, both readers in ONE process so machine load and JIT warmth cancel; three runs, medians, six images from 48 KiB to 4320 KiB of `.text` (90x) and 15504 to 1151779 instructions (74x): sweep 2.6-4.2x, `detectFunctions` 2.0-3.6x** (2.5-3.6x on the two least-loaded runs; the 2.0 is one reading on a 60 KiB image whose absolute time is tens of milliseconds). Equivalence at that scale is the real result and it is **exact**: `sweepX86`'s stream identical element for element and field for field, and the whole `DetectResult` deep-equal, on all six images in all three runs. Wall clocks came off a machine shared with another agent — read the ratios, never the digits.
- **The ARM64 sweep is shared across one load's RPCs, and the key is the bytes.** A64 has no recursive descent — the linear sweep *is* the disassembly — so `detectFunctions` (`bl` targets, switch dispatches), `hybridDisassemble` (the view) and `buildAllXrefs` (adrp pairs) each need the same instructions, and each used to decode the whole section for itself: 3 sweeps, 4083 `cs.disasm` calls and 7.32 MiB fed to Capstone per load of t64-arm.exe. Handing one RPC's `Instruction[]` to the next is **not** the fix — every element carries a `bytes` view, which is the case `workers/transfer.ts` exists to keep out of a message (500k of them measured at 80.6 s to transfer against 1.6 s to clone) — so they share it inside the worker, via `WorkerState.arm64Sweep` (`Arm64SweepCache`). Measured 4083 → 1361 calls and 407 → 166 ms on t64-arm.exe, 3393 → 1131 and 328 → 129 ms on w64-arm.exe (at `7082e66`; see `docs/architecture.md`). **The CALL COUNTS are now re-derived and gated** — `npm run corpus:arm64` drives the three RPCs through the real `dispatch` twice, once sharing the cache and once clearing it before each call, and reports exactly 4083 → 1361 and 3393 → 1131 at `87a8499`, with both gates at 0: the answers must be byte-identical either way, and the shared run must save calls or the audit is measuring a cache that never hits. The *milliseconds* are not re-derived and no ARM64 timing has been taken on this tree. Three things make it safe and none is optional. **The key is the section's bytes**, compared byte-for-byte, not its address or its length: both real ARM64 binaries base `.text` at 0x140001000, so an address key is unsound on the only two files there are to test with, and a *content* key needs no assumption about message order — which matters because a decode can be serviced before the `configure` that announces its file (peek-a-bin-x4o2). Clearing on `configure` is memory hygiene, not correctness. **Only the decode is cached**; `comment` (resolved against the session's string/IAT maps) and `source` (from `.pdata`) are reapplied per caller by `decorateArm64Sweep`, ~5 ms on 27428 instructions plus 2.5 ms for the reference pass the comments now need, so what every RPC returns is unchanged. **A refused section is never stored**: `sweepArm64` throws `Arm64DecodeRateError` before the entry is written, so the ARM64EC/ARM64X refusal cannot decay into a cached empty answer. The plain `disassemble` RPC is deliberately *not* routed through it — it is the one method that may be handed a sub-range, and the cache holds a single section, so a one-function decode would evict the `.text` the other three share (peek-a-bin-kis).
- **`mapInsn`'s comment resolution is an x86 operand grammar, and ARM64 passes it the EMPTY maps.** It asks `resolveRipTarget` for a `[rip ± 0x..]` target and, failing that, scans the operand string for any `0x…` literal that is a known string or IAT address. Both are sound on x86 — an operand there really can carry an absolute address (`mov eax, [0x404000]`, `push 0x40a010`). Neither is sound on A64, where the only literals in an operand are a branch target and an `adrp` **page base**, so on ARM64 the first never fired and only the second could: an instruction was annotated exactly when a literal *coincided* with a data address. Measured at `91085f3`, **248 of t64-arm.exe's 248 comments and 253 of w64-arm.exe's 253 were coincidences, 0 justified** — and it is systematic, not freak, because the IAT starts at a page boundary, so **243 and 252 of them said the same one wrong thing** (`KERNEL32.dll!GetStartupInfoW`, the first import in the page) while the paired `ldr` loaded something else. `decorateArm64Sweep` now annotates from **`findArm64AddressRefs`**, attributed to the instruction that COMPLETES the pair, which is `buildArm64Xrefs`' rule — so an instruction carries a string comment precisely when it is a string xref source, and one grammar answers both. 248 → 275 and 253 → 290 comments, distinct texts 2 → 146 and 2 → 157, the before and after sets **disjoint** (peek-a-bin-vg3). Four things not to undo:
  - **The empty maps are the mechanism, not a tidy-up.** `mapInsn` is *declined* rather than filtered afterwards, which is what makes the x86 path structurally unmovable — `corpus/comments.ts` reports the four x86 comment digests identical and `npm run corpus` byte-identical over all 1127 functions. Passing the real maps back is the defect returning.
  - **`driverMode` is still passed**, so the IOCTL annotation — a shape test over immediates, not a reference resolution — is unchanged on both architectures, and a resolved reference overrides it exactly as a string or IAT hit did before.
  - **The recomputation is on the DECORATION side.** It is a function of the caller's maps, so it belongs beside `comment` and `source` and never inside `Arm64SweepCache` (2.5/4.5 ms against a ~130 ms sweep).
  - **The branch-target half of the mechanism is real but occurs 0 times here.** `extractStrings` scans the code section too, so a `.text` address *can* be in the string map; none of this corpus's branch targets collide. The whole measured population is `adrp` page bases, so do not read the bead's `b #0x140001210` example as something this corpus demonstrates.

  **The suite had pinned the defect as the rule** in two places, both asserting that a lone `adrp` whose page base is in the string map gets that string — same failure mode as `peek-a-bin-qb2x` and `peek-a-bin-92yy`, and why nothing failed for as long as they stood. `corpus/comments.ts` is what catches it now; nothing in `npm run corpus` can, because a comment reaches neither the emitted C nor the IR.
- **A call clobbers what the callee writes, and that answer is only ever ADDED to the narrow one.** `clobberedByCall` (`decompile/ssa.ts`) unions two pieces of evidence and must never substitute one for the other: the argument registers the *call site* was read as passing, and `IRCall.clobbers` — the volatile registers `disasm/callSummary.ts` says the *callee* modifies, closed over the call graph. Modelling a call as destroying the whole Windows x64 volatile set was tried and is **worse**: `__chkstk` preserves everything but RAX/R10/R11 by documented contract, MSVC parks live values in R10 across calls to helpers it has analysed, and clobbering renamed 17 reads of `t64!sub_14000D8C4`'s own parameters and **deleted a guard outright** in `t64!sub_140004A9C` (peek-a-bin-hj1). So the summary is built to **under-approximate**: an unrecognised mnemonic writes nothing, a `pop r` matched by a `push r` is a save/restore rather than a write, and an import, an indirect call or a jump into unrecovered code contributes nothing at all. A write it misses costs a clobber — today's behaviour; a write it invents is the harm. Three consequences worth knowing:
  - **Recursion needs no special case, and inventing one would be less conservative.** `summary(f) = writes(f) ∪ ⋃ summary(callees)` is a union over a finite lattice, so the worklist reaches its least fixpoint; for a cycle that is exactly the union of its members' own writes. Collapsing a strongly-connected component to the ABI set would report writes no member performs.
  - **It is x64 only.** On x86 nothing is passed in a register, so `clobberedByCall` reports nothing at all and every register this could add would be new; the two PE32 binaries are the control and their emitted C is byte-identical. `calleeClobbersFor` in `lifter.ts` is where that gate lives.
  - **`RegState.invalidateCallerSaved` must NOT be narrowed with this summary.** Its soundness runs the other way: it deletes the *expression* recorded for a register, and keeping a stale expression across a call that really did clobber it is a wrong-value defect (`peek-a-bin-lh6`, `peek-a-bin-urs`). It needs an over-approximation; this summary is an under-approximation by construction. Narrowing it soundly would need the closure to also record whether a function's transitive callee set contained *any* unresolved target, and no such flag exists.

  **The browser builds it in the `decompileFunction` RPC, not at disassembly time, and that is what dissolves the race the bead feared** (`peek-a-bin-s2ws`). The premise on file was that the worker's `decompileFunction` "is handed one function's instructions", so a summary must arrive out of band from either `configureDecompileMaps` (before any disassembly exists) or a post-`hybridDisassemble` message (racing the first decompile *and* the client's address-keyed decompile cache, which would then serve a summary-less answer all session). **That is true of the MCP path and false of this one**: `useDecompileTabs` passes the *whole* section's `Instruction[]` — the array `hybridDisassemble` returned — plus every detected function, on every decompile call. So the summary is derived from the same message that consumes it: no sender, no ordering dependency, and no way for a pre-summary entry to enter the cache. Four things are load-bearing:
  - **The client sends extents, not records.** `funcEntries` already crosses but carries *display names*, so it is rebuilt on every rename; `funcExtents` (`[address, size]` pairs) does not change under a rename, which is what lets the summary be cached while `funcEntries` cannot be. `funcInsns.ts`'s `FuncExtent` is the widened parameter type that makes this possible without synthesising a fake `DisasmFunction`.
  - **The cache key is a token minted from array identity, never a content hash.** Structured clone destroys identity across the hop and hashing an instruction array costs about what the build does, so `disasmClient` keeps a `WeakMap<Instruction[], number>` plus a counter that **never resets** — not even on `setImage`. Exactness matters in one direction only: an in-place hex patch can leave the instruction count and end addresses untouched while changing a mnemonic, and a stale summary is a clobber the machine does not perform, which is the one direction this module is built never to guess in. Because tokens are never reused, `CallSummaryCache.clear()` is hygiene rather than correctness — the same status `Arm64SweepCache.clear()` has.
  - **Two gates both mean "byte-identical to the pre-summary path".** No extents sent, or not a 64-bit image: `calleeClobbersFor` returns undefined unless `is64`, so building a summary a PE32 lift cannot consult is pure cost. `npm run corpus` output is byte-identical before and after, which is the proof the `FileSession` path was not touched.
  - **Rebuilding per request is the thing being avoided.** The summary is closed over the call graph and is therefore a property of the whole image, while a request is about one function — measured at ~18 ms over 16.8k instructions, two thirds of it the per-instruction `writtenRegs` scan rather than the fixpoint, on a worker that services messages serially.
- **ARM64 publishes its recovered switch tables' byte extents, and the masking is on the DECORATION side.** `findArm64JumpTables` (`arm64.ts`) reads the `adr` / scaled-load / `add` / `br` chain and recovers **14 tables / 350 cases on t64-arm.exe and 10 / 222 on w64-arm.exe**; it returned only the case lists, `detectArm64Functions` returned `jumpTableSpans: []` one screen below it, and nothing told `dataView.ts` those bytes were a table — so **9 of 255 and 9 of 139 words in recovered table extents rendered as instructions**, one of them a `cbz x8, #0x13ff237c0` aiming outside the image. All 0 now, and the row is a GATE (`peek-a-bin-gb40`, measured at `84eed6e`). Six things:
  - **The cost was the VIEW and not the decode**, which is the whole difference from the x86 analogues (`peek-a-bin-y1di`, `peek-a-bin-mk42`, `peek-a-bin-xqxy`): A64 has no gap fill, so the fixed-width sweep decodes every word of the section whatever any span says and no instruction was ever invented or eaten. What the spans do here is *withhold* words from the answer, which is why the fix has two halves — publishing the extents and consuming them — and why each is separately negative-controlled at 9/9.
  - **The span is the extent `readArm64Table` actually READ, never the one the bounds check claimed.** The read stops at the first entry failing its range or alignment test, so `Arm64Dispatch.count` can overstate it, and marking the unread tail as data would delete real instructions from the view — the direction this project refuses in. `Arm64JumpTables.spans` is sized by `targets.length`, and `corpus/arm64.ts` judges the same quantity so the two cannot disagree. **On both binaries every table reads to its bound**, so this is a bound on the rule and not a saving: `build/arm64Audit.test.ts` and `arm64.test.ts` hold it, and the corpus cannot.
  - **Marking is by BYTE RANGE and the test is intersection, not containment.** One recovered extent is `0x1400018b0`, 33 entries of width 1, so it ends at `0x1400018d1` and its last word owns exactly one table byte; a word holding any table byte did not start where an instruction starts. The only case a *point* test would miss is a span sitting strictly inside one word (≥2 entries of width 1 at an unaligned base), which does not occur here — the corpus is green either way, measured — so `arm64.test.ts`'s "drops a word a span sits strictly inside" is the only thing holding it.
  - **Two dispatches legitimately share one table** — t64-arm's `br 0x140001a34` and `br 0x140001db0` both read `0x140001df0`, over 17 entries and 8 — so spans are deduped by byte range, the x86 `recordSpans` rule. Two reads of one base at *different* lengths keep both entries: their union is the marked region either way, and `rangeTest`'s running-maximum handles overlap, so merging them would be a second rule with nothing to decide.
  - **The filter is in `decorateArm64Sweep`, never in `sweepArm64` or the cache.** The spans are a fact the *caller* supplies, exactly as `pdataRanges` are; filtering the decode would make `Arm64SweepCache` unsound, since two callers with different spans share one entry. Measured: the cache differential is unmoved at 4083 → 1361 and 3393 → 1131.
  - **What moved, and what did not.** Instructions 27428 → 27419 and 24393 → 24384 — exactly the 9 per binary. Three report rows moved with it and all three are the same fact from different directions: `wild branch outside every extent` **1/1 → 0/0** (that branch *was* `0x140001df0`), `unreachable decoded word in a .pdata extent` **2/2 → 1/1** (it was `0x1400018b8`), and `.pdata words that do not decode` **169/111 → 171/113**, rising because two words per binary are inside both a `.pdata` extent and a table. Functions stay 539/494: detection reads the raw sweep, and the `bl` scan is deliberately *not* filtered — it runs in the same pass that finds the tables, and none of the 18 withheld words is a `bl`.
- **A PC-relative `LDR (literal)` is the ISA marking its own data, and on A64 that is the ONLY data-marking rule here that needs no inference.** `ldr q1, #0x1400016f8` carries a signed 19-bit *word* offset and states the datum's width in its destination register — 4 bytes for `w`/`s`, 8 for `x`/`d`, 16 for `q` — so the pool is read off one instruction. It matters more on A64 than it would on x86 because there is no gap fill: the fixed-width sweep decodes every word of the section whatever it holds, so a pool word that happens to be a valid encoding renders as a plausible instruction. t64-arm.exe's `strlen` ends `ldr q1, #0x1400016f8` over the 16-byte lane vector `0f 0e 0d … 01 00`, and its third word decoded as `stxrb w9, w11, [x16]` — **inside a `.pdata` extent, so `source: "recursive"`, i.e. presented as linker-vouched code**. `findArm64LiteralPools` (`arm64Operands.ts`) is the grammar and `literalPoolTest` (`arm64.ts`) applies it. **4 words withheld per binary, all 0 now** (`peek-a-bin-qiws`, `peek-a-bin-56q` item 2, measured at `cc70fe6`). Seven things:
  - **The pools are NOT a caller's fact and are derived inside `decorateArm64Sweep` from `raw` itself**, which is the one place this departs from `peek-a-bin-gb40`'s shape. A jump-table span is something detection *found* and hands over; a literal pool is a property of the very stream being decorated, so every caller must get the same answer and threading it through `DetectResult` would only create a forwarding step to fall out of step — which is precisely the defect gb40's second negative control demonstrates. It also reaches the callers a field would miss: `mcp/disasm.ts`'s xref fallback sweep is filtered now, where gb40 had to leave it alone and say so.
  - **`sweepArm64` and `Arm64SweepCache` are still untouched, and that invariant is what matters.** The raw decode is shared between the three RPCs of a load; only what is *presented* differs. The cache differential is unmoved at 4083 → 1361 and 3393 → 1131, and `arm64.test.ts` pins that the cached sweep still holds every word it read, pool included.
  - **A load sitting inside another load's pool is not honoured.** Pool bytes are arbitrary and one may decode as `ldr x0, #0x…`; a real load is an instruction, so it is in nobody's pool. One pass and not a fixpoint, deliberately — re-admitting a load once its coverer is dropped could only mark MORE, and short is the direction to err. **0 occurrences on both binaries**, so it is a bound held by `arm64.test.ts` alone: removing it fails exactly that one test and moves no corpus row.
  - **Two refusals, both erring short and both with 0 population.** A misaligned target is not parsed, because the immediate counts words so a real target is 4-byte aligned and a misaligned one means the operand was misread; and `prfm <prfop>, <label>` is refused although it shares the encoding class, because it names a cache line rather than a datum and any width chosen for it would be invented. Held by `arm64Operands.test.ts`.
  - **`source` was judged separately and deliberately NOT changed.** The tempting move is to stop claiming `"recursive"` for an in-extent word one suspects of being data. Refused: the `.pdata` extent really is the linker's record that those bytes belong to a function, so the claim is true about the extent, and downgrading on suspicion would replace the one honest signal the field carries on A64 with a heuristic that dims 25336 words to make one honest. Where a word is *provably* data the answer is to withhold it, which is what this does; where it is not, the extent's vouching is not a false statement.
  - **All four withheld words per binary were read against the bytes**, since the corpus cannot adjudicate a data claim: t64-arm 0x1400016f8+16 is the lane vector above, 0x140003858+8 and 0x140003860+8 are the 64-bit constants 0x2b992ddfa232 and …33, and 0x14001ae00+8 is the double 0x3ea8a93728719535. Three of the four are *outside* every `.pdata` extent, which is corroboration from a second direction — a shared constant between functions is exactly where a compiler puts one. Independently: **0 of the 66 distinct pools covers a word any root names** (a branch target, a `.pdata` begin, a detected function, a jump-table case or an `adrp`/`adr` reference), 0 targets are misaligned and 0 fall outside the section.
  - **What moved.** Instructions **27419 → 27415** and **24384 → 24380**, exactly the 4 per binary; `unreachable decoded word in a .pdata extent` **1/1 → 0/0** and now a GATE, its liveness `flanked` 5 → 4; `.pdata words that do not decode` **171/113 → 172/114**, rising by the one withheld word that is inside an extent, which is the honest counterpart and not a regression. Functions stay 539/494 and jump tables 14/10 — **detection reads the raw sweep**, so none of this reaches it. `corpus:comments` unmoved at 275/275 and 290/290 justified with 146/157 distinct texts. x86 **byte-identical**: `diff -r` over the whole artifact tree finds only the label line and the artifact path, `compare.mjs` "byte-identical throughout" with `CHANGED 0 / only-base 0 / only-change 0`.
- **The ARM64 stack frame comes out of `.pdata`, and the packed `FrameSize` field means the FRAME DELTA rather than the total allocation.** `analyzeStackFrame` is an x86 operand grammar that refuses A64 (`peek-a-bin-56q`); `disasm/arm64Frame.ts` is the **second grammar**, and it arrives at the answer from a completely different place — the linker already wrote it down. Every ARM64 `.pdata` entry carries an unwind record stating whether `x29` is a frame pointer and how far below entry `sp` it sits, which is the fact x86 spends `inlineFrameGeometry`, `frameGeometry` and a `__SEH_prolog4` helper walk recovering. `pe/arm64Unwind.ts` decodes both encodings — the packed `.pdata` word (bits 13-15 `RegF`, 16-19 `RegI`, 20 `H`, 21-22 `CR`, 23-31 `FrameSize`/16) and the `.xdata` unwind CODES — and `disasm/stackFrame.ts` is the one place that dispatches between the two grammars. **Measured over all 800 records of `t64-arm.exe` and `w64-arm.exe`: 419/419 and 381/381 decode, and the record agrees with an independently written reading of the prologue on 376/338 frame deltas and on all 800 "is there a frame pointer at all" — 0 disagreements in either direction**, gated in `corpus/arm64.ts` row 10. Seven things, and the first two are corrections to the bead that filed this (`peek-a-bin-hof0`):
  - **THE BEAD'S ACCOUNT OF THE FRAME SIZE IS WRONG AND THE RIGHT RULE IS SIMPLER.** It recorded a crude total-allocation reader agreeing with packed `FrameSize` on 254/263 and 227/237 and called the residue "MSVC's TWO-PART frame … the 16-byte gap is the CR=3 fp/lr pair, which the packed field accounts for separately". Re-derived here: the residual gap is **not** 16 bytes (`w64-arm!0x1400025a8` has 0x20) and it is **not** the fp/lr pair — it is the area MSVC allocates *below* the frame pointer with a `sub sp, sp, #N` after `mov x29, sp`. The quantity `FrameSize` actually states is `E - x29`, **exactly, on all 496 chained packed entries**, which is `StackFrame.frameDelta`'s own definition. There is no two-part accounting to model; a total-allocation reader was simply measuring the wrong thing.
  - **An area allocated BELOW the frame pointer is outside every unwind record, in BOTH encodings**, and that is by design rather than an omission: a chained unwinder restores `sp` from `x29` and never needs it. 9 of 263 and 10 of 237 packed entries have one, plus the `bl __chkstk` shape (`t64-arm!0x14000568c` — `stp`/`mov x29,sp`/`bl`/`sub sp, sp, #0x4b0`) where the `.xdata` codes stop short too. So `frameSize` is a **lower bound** for a chained function and exact for an unchained one, reported and not gated.
  - **`frameSize` is the record's TOTAL and `frameDelta` is the delta, and conflating them was a live defect caught by READING one recovered frame.** MSVC's `add_fp` shape puts `x29` near the *top* of the frame, so `t64-arm!sub_140001070` has a delta of 0x10 and allocates 0x60; reporting the delta as the size understated it by 80 bytes while the function's own slots ran out to +0x28. **No gate sees it** — the corpus run is 51 of 51 green with the defect in place, and only two report rows move — so `arm64Unwind.test.ts` is the whole instrument for it.
  - **A NEGATIVE delta is refused, and it is x86's own rule reached from the A64 side.** `t64-arm!0x140001830` and its `w64-arm` twin allocate nothing and then say `add x29, sp, #0x10`, putting the frame register sixteen bytes *above* entry `sp` — a stack-probe thunk fragment running on and writing into its caller's frame. That is precisely what `stack.ts`'s `addressesOwnFrame` refuses on x86 for `__SEH_prolog4` (`peek-a-bin-s7hl`). Publishing it puts the frame-pointer gate red at exactly 1 per binary, which is the negative control.
  - **TWO BUGS IN THE UNWIND-CODE TABLE WERE CAUGHT BY THE CORPUS AND NOT BY READING**: `save_r19r20_x` allocates `z * 8`, not `(z + 1) * 16`, and `add_fp`'s displacement is **subtracted** from the delta because it moves `x29` up toward entry `sp`. With either wrong, agreement with the instruction stream is 85 of 156 rather than 156 of 156 (70 and 3 wrong deltas on t64-arm). The codes also run **backwards through the prologue** — the first byte is the last prologue instruction — which is the whole of the delta arithmetic, and 112 of t64-arm's 156 records carry more bytes *after* `end` that must not be counted.
  - **The var list is the one half with NO oracle, and it is reported rather than gated.** `frameDelta` comes from `.pdata` alone, which is what makes the stream an oracle for it; a var list is checked against nothing, since the only other record of which bytes are locals is the stream it is read from. 1906 and 1669 slots over 403 and 364 functions; **0 at a negative offset** (that branch is a bound, not a saving — MSVC lays the A64 frame out upward); and 122 and 95 lying at or beyond the recorded frame size, adjudicated to exactly the two legitimate shapes above (29 of 30 contain a `bl`, the other is the caller's-frame thunk). Three exclusions in the operand grammar are each the difference between a slot and something else: pre-index **writeback** (`[sp, #-0x20]!`) is the prologue *moving* `sp` and counting it reports 683 and 617 spurious negative slots; a **register offset** names no constant slot; and an `ldp`/`stp` touches **two** slots, which has no x86 analogue.
  - **The SIGNATURE was refused on evidence, not left undone.** `.pdata` carries no arity information — the only field that could bear on it is `H` ("the prologue homes x0-x7"), which is **0 on all 500 packed entries** — and AAPCS64 passes eight arguments in registers with no home space, so there are **0** frame accesses at or above `frameDelta` in either binary and any positional rule would gate on an empty population. The x86 rule read across (count reads of x0-x7 preceding any write) is a real inference with **no oracle**: no A64 emitted C, no `apitypes.ts` entry for a `sub_` callee. `inferSignature` therefore still returns null, and the panel says nothing rather than a count nothing can check.

  **Only ONE call site was converted, and that is a decision.** `mcp/tools.ts`, `hooks/useDecompileTabs.ts` and `llm/decompileForLLM.ts` each build a `StackFrame` only to hand it to `decompileFunction`, and the decompiler refuses A64 above them — so they keep calling `analyzeStackFrame` directly and are x86-only by construction. Routing them through the dispatcher would be inert at best and, if that refusal ever moved, would hand an x86 IR lifter a frame recovered under another architecture's rules. `DisassemblyView`'s detail panel is the one surface an A64 frame can reach — and **it is also why the vars had to land with the frame**: the panel renders "No variables detected" for an empty list, so a frame without them would have installed a *new* falsehood where `peek-a-bin-56q` had just removed one.

- **In every arch dispatch, the `"unsupported"` arm must be checked *before* the `"arm64"` arm.** `dispatch.ts` and `mcp/disasm.ts` branch on `state.arch` in a chain, and the tail of that chain is the x86 path. Put the ARM64 test first and an unsupported image falls straight through to x86 — which is the exact bug the third state exists to fix: an ARMNT file decoded as x86 produces a full screen of plausible instructions the file does not contain, and unlike ARM64 there is no coverage signal to notice it by, because an x86 linear sweep decodes essentially any byte string. `WorkerState.arch`'s docstring says this too; keep both true.
- **`if (c) { continue; } break;` at the bottom of a loop body is emitted as `if (!c) { break; }`, and every restriction on that is a position where the fallthrough means something else.** `structure.ts`'s `armFrom` gives a body-bottom conditional both of its arms — `continue` for the back edge, `break`/`goto` for the exit — and the pair makes the reader hold both in mind to learn one thing. **135 collapsed of 137** over the four corpus binaries at `f3b89ec` (32/38/35/30 on t32/t64/w64/w32, from 33/38/35/31), one line saved at each. `collapseLoopTailContinue` (`cleanup.ts`) is the pass; it is cosmetic and identical in every loop construct, since `continue` and falling off the end of a body both reach the back edge — the update then the test in a `for`, the test in a `while` or `do/while`. Six things:
  - **The pair must be the last two statements of a LOOP's own body**, which is why it walks loops explicitly instead of going through `rewriteBodies`. In a `switch` arm `break` leaves the switch and the fallthrough falls into the next case; inside a nested `if` the fallthrough is the rest of the loop body. Both are different programs, both are pinned, and both fail under the negative control that hands the rewrite every body in the tree.
  - **The negation must be a FLIPPED COMPARISON, never a `!`-wrapping.** `RegState.negate` falls back to `irUnary("!", …)` for anything it cannot flip, so `if (!!__unrecovered_1)` would become `if (!!!__unrecovered_1)` — less direct than the two-armed form it replaces. That is the whole of the 137 → 135 residue: one site per PE32 binary, 0 on x64. `&&`/`||` are refused too (De Morgan is correct and harder to read; 0 occurrences).
  - **The exit arm keeps its own spelling** — a `break` stays a `break`, a `goto L` stays a `goto L` — and the pass runs **after `breakForwardGotos`**, or a quarter of the population (7/12/10/7) is still spelled `goto` and is missed.
  - **The braces stay, deliberately, and that refusal is measured.** The bead sketches `if (c) break;`, and `corpus/sweep.ts`'s guard scan matches an `if` only when the line ends in `{` — so one-lining the shape in `emit.ts` would take **all 2077** single-terminator-body guards in this corpus out of that scan, for a brace-style rule this pass has no business deciding. Many of those *are* anchored and judged today (a body `return eax;` carries an address; a `break;` does not).
  - **These 137 guards were never in the polarity audit's population**, which is why the rewrite is safe and also why nothing in the corpus suite would have caught a wrong negation: `emitStmt` pushes `break`/`continue` with no address, so `bodyAddrAt` answers `body-has-no-address` and an `if` has no anchor B. The oracle is `cleanup.test.ts` plus hand-reading — `t32!sub_401884` 0x40194c is `test eax,eax / jne 0x401935` with the back edge taken and the fallthrough at `loc_40194E`, so `if (eax == 0) { break; }` is the machine (verified against `objdump -d -M intel`).
  - **Measured at base `f3b89ec` + `peek-a-bin-l1f`, both sides pinned: exactly ONE row moves.** Emitted lines 16479/17056/15389/15013 → 16447/17018/15354/14983, i.e. the 135 collapses and nothing else. **Loop shape identical** — `if` 1987/1938/1719/1759, `while` (raw) 189/183/175/175, `do/while` 78/77/76/77, `for` 6/6/6/6, which is what bead 252 required of it — line-map coverage **unmoved** (none of the four lines carried an address), invented prelude declarations unmoved at 1460/2456/2278/1417, `polarity guards audited` unmoved with `CHANGED 0 / only-base 0 / only-change 0`, and every gate flat (`peek-a-bin-252`).
- **A block with no predecessor is not necessarily dead code.** `structureCFG`'s leftover pass used to require reachability from the entry, on the reasoning that everything else is alignment padding. What that excluded was 1160 blocks of real code: an MSVC `__except`/`__finally` continuation or a 32-bit SEH scope handler is entered *by the unwinder*, so it has no predecessor at all and sits past a `ret`, while being ordinary code inside the function's own bounds. 29 real calls never reached the output. Padding is still excluded, by the test that was already doing the work — a block that lifts to no statements is not resurrected. Cost of the fix: +20.1% emitted text on t32, +0.1% on the two x64 binaries (`peek-a-bin-d3z`).
- **…and "lifts to no statements" stopped being able to identify padding the moment step 4b started hoisting branches out.** A `cmp eax, 0x64 / jg` block lifts to exactly one statement — the branch — and `pipeline.ts` step 4b takes it, leaving an empty list that the leftover pass above read as alignment. So a *test the machine makes* was dropped whenever this pass was the only route to the block: **81 blocks, 55 on t32 and 26 on w32**, every one of them a guard in MSVC's format-specifier dispatch chains, where `armBody` claims the head of the chain as a one-block switch arm and leaves the rest to be resurrected here. The test is now `… === 0 && !branches.has(b.id)`, because `branches.has` *is* "step 4b took a statement out of this block" — and it is asked of the block rather than of its instructions, so a jcc the lifter deliberately **refused** to model still counts as contributing nothing (`peek-a-bin-3zji`).

  **What was dropped was the condition, never the code**, and that distinction is the whole reason this is a smaller defect than `d3z` was: 0 statements and 0 calls were lost on all four binaries, because each of the 81 blocks lifts to no statement of its own and the successors they guard were each resurrected as their own `loc_` region. The reader was told *what* happened and never *when* — `t32!sub_4045B1` emitted `case 7:` computing a value and doing nothing, with `eax > 0x64`, `eax == 0x64`, `eax > 0x53` and `eax == 0x53` appearing nowhere in its 683 lines. x64 is untouched because it recovers no jump tables at all, so `armBody` never runs there and no dispatch chain is orphaned.

  **No gate could see it, and each is blind for its own structural reason** — worth knowing, because it is the clearest example in this repo of a defect falling between instruments. The statement-drop audit snapshots *after* step 4b (deliberately, or every branch reads as dropped), so a block whose only statement *was* the branch has an empty list on both sides and cannot register. Polarity only judges guards that exist, so a missing guard leaves the denominator and the ratio stays 1.00. `staleGuards` counts wrong readings, not absent ones; `unrecovered` counts admissions, and these were silent rather than admitted; gcc compiles a shorter function happily. The instrument that finds it in one line is **the count of blocks left unvisited at the end of `structureCFG`, split by whether `branches` holds an entry** — that separates correctly-skipped padding from a dropped test, and the second half is gateable at 0. It is 0 on all four binaries now (t32 57 → 2 unvisited, w32 29 → 3, the survivors being an `int3` run, a `jmp` whose target is already emitted, and `nop` padding). Measured: **+42 guards judged on t32 and +20 on w32** (+1 each on t64/w64), `if` emitted 1907 → 1962 and 1721 → 1747, `CHANGED 0` and `only-base 0` everywhere, loop shapes identical, every other gate flat. The residue where `armBody` claims an arm block *and* under-emits it — 25 blocks on t32, 12 on w32 — was a different mechanism and is fixed below (`peek-a-bin-pqs5`).

- **…and a switch arm that ends in a test must say so: `break` is a claim about control flow, not a terminator you can always append.** `armBody` claims one block and closed it with `break` however that block ends, which for a block ending in a conditional jump is false twice over — the switch does not end there, and **the condition goes with it**, since step 4b has already hoisted the `IRBranch` out of `liftedBlocks` and nothing else ever asks the block what it tested. `t32!sub_4045B1` case 7 was `eax = (uint16_t)ecx; break;` for the block `movzx eax, cx / cmp eax, 0x64 / jg 0x404B46`, with `eax > 0x64` nowhere in its 698 lines and **no `goto` anywhere naming either successor**, so `loc_404917` and `loc_404B46` sat in the output as regions the emitted C can never reach. `armExit` (`structure.ts`) now spells the block's own exit: `if (cond) goto <taken>;` then `goto <fallthrough>;` for a conditional jump, `goto <succ>` for an unconditional one, and `break` only for a block with no successors. **35 arm blocks on t32 and 17 on w32 closed with `break` while having a successor**; of those, **31 and 14 were a false claim** — 25 and 12 ending in a conditional jump, where the condition was lost as well, plus 6 and 2 whose `jmp` goes to another arm or to the `default` body, where `break` skips code the machine runs. The other 4 and 3 jump to the block that follows the switch, where `break` said the same thing. Two decisions are load-bearing:
  - **The transfer is spelled, not followed.** `armFrom`'s doctrine — a `goto` to the target's label is faithful whatever the target is — and it keeps this out of the switch's convergence scan: no extra block is claimed, so the guard ledger is *provably* flat (`CHANGED 0`, `only-base 0`, `only-change 0` on all four binaries). The bead's suggested fix, giving `armBody` `structureFrom`'s treatment, was measured and refused: naively it **empties every arm**, because the arm's own block is in `switchStopAt` so `structureFrom` bails at once (t32's eight cases each became a bare `break;` with the statements relocated, and *every gate stayed green*); with `enterStart` it recovers the same 37 conditions as this fix and additionally takes 8 guards on t32 and 4 on w32 **out** of the polarity audit's anchored set, and it still appends an unconditional `break` wherever its walk stops, so the same false claim recurs one block further on.
  - **A `goto` where `break` was already right is noise, never a claim.** At 7 of the 15 single-successor arms the successor is the block that follows the switch anyway, and `armBody` cannot know that without walking. Uniformity is the honest trade — "not a case target, therefore the join" is a heuristic with no evidence, of the kind `computePostDominators` exists to avoid. One stage-level expectation in `structure.test.ts` moved for exactly this reason and its comment says so.

  Measured at `91085f3`: `if` emitted **1962 → 1987 (t32)** and **1747 → 1759 (w32)**, x64 byte-identical; 4 of 288 and 2 of 285 functions changed; gotos 788 → 848 and 708 → 737 with **labels unmoved** (735/647 — every target was already emitted) and dangling 0; emitted text +77 and +37 lines (+0.5%/+0.25%). The one cost is **unrecovered values 106 → 108 and 87 → 88**: 3 of the 37 conditions cannot be spelled and become an admitted `__unrecovered_N` in a guard that previously said nothing at all. Every gate flat — gcc 842/842, `offsetof` 970/970 across 169, polarity 1506/1506 with **0 inverted** and audited unchanged at 490/543/473/421, 0 throws, 0 statements dropped of 7066/7604/6753/6560, 0 callees lost, 0 loops short, arity exact/under/over unmoved, stale version-0 0 wrong and 0 spoiled, wrong-operand guards 0 named with `shapes` unmoved, pop-restored reads unmoved at 7/0/0/6 — and **loop shapes identical**: `for` 3/5/5/3, `while` 114/107/100/101, `do/while` 78/77/76/77. **The instrument is `armExit`'s own answer, gateable at 0**: arm blocks closed with `break` while having a successor, 35/0/0/17 before and **0 on all four** after. No standing gate could see it, for 3zji's reasons plus one of its own — the statements were all on the page, so the statement-drop audit is exactly right to report 0.
- **Register names follow the image's width, and the phi cannot tell you what that is.** `canonReg` maps every alias to the 64-bit parent because that is the register's *identity*, and SSA keys on identity — so `phi.dest.size` is `regSize("rdi") = 8` for **every** phi even in 32-bit code (measured over every phi t32.exe produced, 741 of them at the time), and a width-based `canonReg` inverse has nothing to invert. Lowering a phi to a copy with the canonical name emitted `rdi = rax` inside a function whose every other line said `edi`: **77 of t32's functions — better than a quarter of them** — named a register the image has no encoding for. `destroySSA` takes the width from the function's own statements instead (`registerSpeller`), falling back to `regAtSize(canon, 4)` where `ssaOptimize` deleted the defining statement after the phi was placed (`peek-a-bin-1k4`).
- **…but the *function* is the wrong scope for that width, and the live range is the right one.** `registerSpeller` kept the widest alias per canonical register and gave it **one name per function**, which cannot serve a register carrying two live ranges of different widths at once — and that is ordinary MSVC output, not a corner case. `mov rbp, r9` / `mov r9d, r14d` is R9's entry value copied away as a pointer and then a 32-bit clobber of R9D; copy propagation forwards `rbp` to `r9`, so the stores are emitted against a 64-bit read of the entry value while the 32-bit range gets a phi copy — and asking the function gives *both* the name `r9`. Six stores plus a `rax = r9` then went through a pointer the emitted C had already reassigned, while two guards read an `r9d` nothing assigned: a write through the wrong pointer and a read of an unassigned name, in C that compiles clean, because `gcc` declares `r9` and `r9d` as two unrelated `long`s. The fix is **per-live-range naming**, not more evidence — both ranges survive and one name cannot serve both. A live range here is a *phi web*: the versions a phi ties together transitively, restricted to one canonical register (after `ssaOptimize` an operand may name a different register, and that is a real cross-register copy whose two sides are spelled from their own ranges). The web takes the widest mention of its **own** members, which is what its readers already say; the function-wide answer is the fallback for a web `ssaOptimize` left with no mention at all, which is what keeps `peek-a-bin-1k4` fixed. Narrowing can only help — a web is then named by a spelling some member uses, where the function-wide answer may be one *no* member uses. `nameClobberedReads` and the phi copy's `clobber` branch build the same `clobbered_…` name, so both pass the version or neither may. Measured at `82ed61e`: emitted C changed in 4/288, 43/279, 3/285 and 41/275 functions (t32/t64/w32/w64), gcc/offsetof/polarity/callees/statement-drops/**arity all unmoved**, and the stale-read gate green at 0 over a widened site set (`peek-a-bin-pzws`).
- **…and the phi's fake *width* is inert, while its canonical *name* leaked through one line of `splitStaleReads`. The census is the result here, not the fix.** `insertPhis` mints every phi as `irReg(canonReg(...))`, so `phi.dest.size` is `regSize` of the 64-bit parent — 8 for every phi in every function, PE32 included — and the operand fill copies that width verbatim. The standing plan was to give the phi a truthful width and spelling in `ssa.ts`, with an A/B "because the fake width feeds folding". **Both halves of that were measured and both are wrong**, so do not spend a session on it (`peek-a-bin-0s6e`):
  - **Only 11 sites in the tree read an `IRReg`'s `.size` at all**, of 140 checker-resolved reads of an `IRReg`'s `.name`/`.size` over `src/` and `corpus/` excluding tests. Resolve it the way the `IRStmt` census above is resolved — the checker, matching the declared interface name `IRReg`, never a `kind` string. One of the 11 is in `regstate.ts`, which is lifter-only and runs before a phi exists; one is the propagator itself (`ssa.ts:679`); six are `ssadestroy.ts`'s own repair machinery; **two are genuinely downstream**, both in `fold.ts` — `knownWidth` via `narrowEnoughForMask32` (`x & 0xffffffff` → `x`) and the same-size cast strip.
  - **The bead's third site, `fold.ts:178`, cannot be one.** It sits inside `if (left.kind === "const" && right.kind === "const")`, so both operands are `IRConst`; no register of any provenance reaches it. A caveat that names a line is still a claim to check.
  - **Neither real site sees a phi-derived register on this corpus.** Instrumented over all four binaries: `narrowEnoughForMask32` is called 384/284/264/356 times (t32/t64/w64/w32) and sees a canonically-named 64-bit register **0 times**; the cast strip is reached with a register operand 224/644/548/176 times and sees one **0 times**. The probe is validated by a control counting *every* register `foldExpr` sees — t32 16 of 68452, w32 8 of 66136 — so it does observe the leak and simply never finds it on a width decision. Both sites also fail *conservatively* today (the mask is kept, the cast is kept), so a truthful width would make folding more aggressive rather than repair anything.
  - **What reached the page was the NAME, by one route.** `splitStaleReads` builds a `spellings` map from its stale rows to answer "was this version read under one spelling throughout", and a **phi operand contributed `op.value.name`** as though it were a read. It is not: it is the canonical identity, and where a version's only stale row was a phi operand the repair variable and the register it copies from were both spelled from it — `rcx_3 = rcx` with `long rcx;` invented in the prelude, inside a 32-bit function saying `ecx` 36 times. The fix is one `continue`: a phi-operand row is evidence only when a later pass substituted a real value into it (`replaceRegInCtx` rewrites operands from copy propagation and GVN), which is exactly when its name is no longer the canonical one. Otherwise it abstains and `spell` — `peek-a-bin-pzws`' per-live-range answer — decides, so the name is still one some member of the web uses.
  - **The population was 4× what the bead recorded**, because the bead counted only *bare* canonical names and the versioned repair variables (`rbx_1`, `rcx_21`) are the same defect from the same line: **124 mentions of 18 distinct names over 19 of 573 PE32 functions at `d514274`, and 21 + 14 invented prelude declarations — all 0 now.** On the x64 pair, where a 64-bit name is an ordinary correct spelling, the same change narrows 80 and 56 prelude declarations and 267 and 186 mentions onto the width their readers use; the nine guards whose text moved were read against `objdump` and every one is the machine's own width (`cmp WORD PTR [r8], cx` → `cx_84` not `rcx_84`, `test r11b, r12b` → `r11b_7`, `test r13b, 0x4` → `r13b_13`).
  - **`IRReg.size` follows the name it carries**, so the repair copy takes `regSize(spelling)`. A no-op for every row that spelled itself, and **byte-identical output on all four binaries with or without it** — it is kept because that invariant is what the two `fold.ts` width readers and `emit.ts`'s `operandWidth` consume, and today's zero at those sites is a fact about this corpus rather than a property.
  - **Negative-controlled in both directions, and the two halves separate cleanly.** Restore the phi operand's spelling evidence and the emitted C is **byte-identical to base** (md5, all four) with the new gate reporting exactly 124; keep the skip and drop the width change and it is **byte-identical to the fix**. Gated by `corpus/emitAudits.ts`'s `unencodableNames` — see below.
- **A register name the image has no encoding for is a gate, and it is the only oracle here that can see a wrong register *name*.** `unencodableNames` (`corpus/emitAudits.ts`), **0 mentions over 573 PE32 functions**, from 124 over 19 functions at `d514274`. It is asked of **PE32 only**, and that restriction is what makes it an oracle: a 32-bit image has no RCX, so every occurrence is provably a name no instruction wrote and no reader can mean — `polarity inverted`'s character rather than a baseline's. On x64 `rcx` is a correct spelling and telling a canonical name from a real 64-bit read needs the live range's own width, which `registerSpeller` answers and the emitted text does not record; so the x64 pair contributes a structural 0 and `funcs` beside it is the liveness half. **`gcc` and `corpus/staleReads.ts` are both blind to this class** — `preludeFor` declares every undeclared identifier as its own `long`, so `rcx` and `ecx` compile as two unrelated variables, and `staleReads` compares the *name* a read uses (deliberately, since a correct live-range split emits two names for one register) so a canonical name reads to it as a legitimate second range. That is why CLAUDE.md's crude read-but-never-assigned scan was the only thing that had ever seen it, as an upper bound (`peek-a-bin-0s6e`).
- **How much of the argument area the frame recovery is still missing is REPORTED**: `offsetNamedArgs` in `corpus/emitAudits.ts`, **0/83/83/0 occurrences over 0/9/9/0 functions** (t32/t64/w64/w32) at `97249dc` + `peek-a-bin-ikd`, from 16/147/141/14 over 8/33/30/7 at `41113c9` + `peek-a-bin-emlv`, itself from 156/147/141/140 over 32/33/30/29. An `arg_0x<N>` whose `(N - firstOffset) % slotSize === 0` is a slot `stack.ts` **would** have indexed had it recognised the frame, so the offset spelling is down to the recognition and to nothing about the file; the sub-slot half (7/5 on the x64 pair, the third byte of an argument) is reported apart because it is correctly offset-named at any level of recovery. **Report-only in both directions and not gateable at 0**: the PE32 residue is detection over-production, where the prologue is outside the range and the frame register is the enclosing function's, and the x64 population was a different defect again — phantom parameters rather than unnamed ones — and `peek-a-bin-ikd` has now taken 53 of t64's 147 and 58 of w64's 141 out of it by *refusing* them as parameters, along with the whole PE32 residue. **`peek-a-bin-sx57` then indexed the ones that are provable and REFUSED the rest, taking it to 49/49 over 8/8 functions with 9 distinct names on the x64 pair (t32/w32 unmoved at 0, byte-identical) — and the residue was then slots that are NOT arguments at all.** 20 of the 35 x64 slots were named (15 above the home space, 5 spilled home slots); the 15 refused are 4+3+3 callee-saved register saves in `sub_1400027C8`/`sub_14000BEE8`/`sub_14000C24C` and 5 byte locals or out-param buffers in unspilled home slots. **`peek-a-bin-g186` then took the row to 0/0/0/0 at `16f1633` by withdrawing those 16 (the 15 plus the framed `sub_140006804`'s `[rbp+0x20]`) as parameters outright** — so the row is 0 everywhere now and is a *dead* instrument on x64 until frame recovery regresses; the docstring in `corpus/emitAudits.ts` still describes the x64 population as live. **Reaching 0 is nevertheless the WRONG target, and that stays true after g186**, because the flattering number is reachable by the opposite change, and that is measured rather than argued: a variant naming all 35 takes it to 0/0/0/0 and moves **no other number in the report**, including `offsetof` fields and distinct definitions, while printing `void sub_1400027C8(int64_t arg_0, …) { arg_0 = rbx; arg_1 = rsi; arg_2 = rdi; arg_3 = r12; }` — four declared parameters immediately overwritten by the callee-saved registers being parked in them. g186 reaches the same 0 by declaring **no** parameter there, so the row cannot tell the two apart and the instrument that can is the emitted parameter list read against `objdump`. Nothing else here sees the class: an offset-named argument is a well-typed name gcc compiles, it states nothing false so polarity/`staleGuards`/`staleReads` are indifferent, it is not an admission so the unrecovered count is unmoved, and `offsetof` only checks layouts it was given — the whole point being that such a slot never reaches struct synthesis as a parameter. Negative-controlled: run over the base's own emitted C it reports exactly 156/147/141/140, agreeing figure for figure with an independently written census (`peek-a-bin-emlv`).
- **ARM64X carries machine 0xAA64 and is refused by decode rate. ARM64EC does NOT carry 0xAA64 — it is marked x64 — and this bullet asserted the opposite for months.** Settled against Microsoft's own documentation, since no such binary exists here (`peek-a-bin-3ucw`): a **final ARM64EC image carries `IMAGE_FILE_MACHINE_AMD64` (0x8664)**, per [Microsoft's Arm64EC overview](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/arm/arm64ec), which shows `link /dump /headers` reporting `8664 machine (x64) (ARM64X)` and states that only the linked EXE or DLL carries "the `8664 (x64) (ARM64X)` or `AA64 (ARM64) (ARM64X)` machine value", and per lld (`case ARM64EC: coff->Machine = AMD64`, [D149086](https://reviews.llvm.org/D149086)); an **ARM64X image carries 0xAA64 by default** — "By default, Arm64X binaries appear to be Arm64 binaries", so Windows 10 on Arm can still load one ([Arm64X PE Files](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/arm/arm64x-pe)) — and may instead be marked 0x8664, which is what makes it launch as emulated x64; and **0xA641 / 0xA64E never appear in a linked image's machine field**, 0xA641 being an object/lib marker Microsoft calls "not a valid final PE machine type" (`dumpbin`'s `(ARM64X)` annotation comes from the hybrid metadata, not from this word). Three consequences, and the second is the one to carry. **The refusal's real population is an ARM64X image marked 0xAA64** plus an image whose machine word is not describing its bytes — it never sees an ARM64EC one, which is a narrower claim than this bullet used to make. **An ARM64EC image, and an ARM64X marked 0x8664, take the `"x86"` arm and are disassembled as x64 with nothing said**, which is a real misclassification and not a benign one: Arm64EC code is *native A64* following x64 software conventions, so the x64 decoder reads it as fiction and an x86 linear sweep decodes essentially any byte string — the same silent failure `arch.ts` records for ARMNT. It is **knowingly left alone**, because routing on CHPE there would be a *decision* taken on a field that has never once been read on an x64 image here: measured at `11408ac`, `t64.exe` and `w64.exe` have **no load-config data directory at all**, so both read `undefined`, and the only two images on this machine that read the field are the genuine ARM64 pair, which read 0. And an ARM64EC image's `.pdata` is read with the **x64** schema, which `peek-a-bin-c71x` has since settled as the **right** reading of the directory the header names and an **incomplete** reading of the image — see the two-exception-tables gotcha below, which is also why `pe/pdata.ts` no longer accepts 0xA641/0xA64E as a superset. The evidence actually used is the bytes: sweeping a code section with an A64 handle decodes 97.4%/97.7% of the two real ARM64 binaries against 21.8–27.9% of four x86/x64 ones. **~A quarter of arbitrary x86 bytes decode as *something* in A64** — that density is exactly why the failure was silent, and why the floor is 50% rather than anything near 100%. `disassembleArm64` throws `Arm64DecodeRateError` below it on sections of 256+ words; `detectArm64Functions` catches and degrades via `DetectResult.omitted`, because `.pdata`, the exports and the unwind handlers are the linker's own record and stay true. **The calibration is now a GATE in both directions** (`npm run corpus:arm64`): `coffHeader.machine` is the oracle, so an accepted 0xAA64 image must be above the floor and every 0x014C/0x8664 image must be below it, and moving the floor either way turns a row red — negative-controlled at 0.2 (the four x86 binaries pass, red) and at 0.99 (both ARM64 images refused, red). The printed table re-derives this docstring's figures exactly and adds w32 at 12288/2743. Stated limitation, unchanged and **not** narrowed by the gate: an ARM64X image is a good share genuine A64 and may pass the floor, and there is none on this machine (`peek-a-bin-2t1`).
- **A HYBRID IMAGE HAS TWO EXCEPTION TABLES, `pe/pdata.ts` READS ONE OF THEM, AND THE ONE IT READS IS THE ONE THE MACHINE WORD DESCRIBES — so machine-keying is right here and could never be right for the other.** Settled against documentation at `peek-a-bin-c71x`, unobserved for the usual reason: there is no ARM64EC or ARM64X binary on this machine. An ARM64EC image holds native A64 code following x64 software conventions **and** genuine x64 code, and each half has its own function table in its own architecture's format:

  | image, as it lies on disk | `IMAGE_DIRECTORY_ENTRY_EXCEPTION` | CHPE `ExtraRFETable` |
  |---|---|---|
  | ARM64EC (marked 0x8664) | **x64**, 12-byte entries | **ARM64**, 8-byte entries |
  | ARM64X (marked 0xAA64, default) | **ARM64**, 8-byte entries | **x64**, 12-byte entries |

  The invariant is that the exception directory always holds the table of the architecture the image *presents itself as*, and `ExtraRFETable` — a field of `IMAGE_ARM64EC_METADATA`, reached through `loadConfig.chpeMetadataPointer` — always holds the other one. For ARM64X the **ARM64X dynamic-value relocations swap both** when the image is loaded into an x64/EC process, so its hybrid view is byte-for-byte an ARM64EC image's layout and its on-disk view — what a static reader sees — is the ARM64 one. Six things:
  - **Three independent sources, none of them a secondary account, and all three read directly.** lld's COFF writer says it outright — `// ARM64EC (but not ARM64X) contains x86_64 exception table in data directory` over `machine == ARM64EC ? hybridPdata : pdata`, where `hybridPdata` is declared `// x86_64 .pdata sections on ARM64EC/ARM64X targets` and `mergeSections` splits `.pdata` in two by `chunk->getMachine() == AMD64`; the same file points `__arm64x_extra_rfe_table` at the *complement* (`machine == ARM64X ? hybridPdata : pdata`) and, for ARM64X, emits `IMAGE_DVRT_ARM64X_FIXUP_TYPE_VALUE` relocations over both the `EXCEPTION_TABLE` data directory and `offsetof(chpe_metadata, ExtraRFETable)` ([Writer.cpp](https://github.com/llvm/llvm-project/blob/main/lld/COFF/Writer.cpp)). Wine's loader routes by **code range** rather than by image: `RtlLookupFunctionTable` answers `ExtraRFETable`/`ExtraRFETableSize` when `RtlIsEcCode(pc)` and `IMAGE_DIRECTORY_ENTRY_EXCEPTION` otherwise, and the x64 `RtlLookupFunctionEntry` delegates an EC pc to the ARM64 one, which searches `ARM64_RUNTIME_FUNCTION` `{BeginAddress, Flag, FunctionLength, UnwindData}` — the 8-byte form `parseArm64Pdata` decodes ([unwind.c](https://github.com/wine-mirror/wine/blob/master/dlls/ntdll/unwind.c)). And Microsoft's [Arm64EC ABI page](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/arm/arm64ec-abi) requires *dynamically* added EC unwind entries to "be in Arm64 format", noting `RUNTIME_FUNCTION` is the x64 shape when compiling EC so `ARM64_RUNTIME_FUNCTION` must be named explicitly; its worked entry-thunk listing is ARM64 unwind codes (`E7 save_any_reg`, `E6 save_next_pair`, `E1`, `E4 end`).
  - **So `parsePdata`'s answer is RIGHT for every hybrid case and INCOMPLETE for all of them.** The directory really does hold what the machine word says — including for both hybrids, in both of ARM64X's views — and the other half of the image's functions is in a table nothing here reads. That is a narrower and more useful statement than `3ucw` could make, which was "whether the x64 path is right is not established".
  - **Reading the second table was deliberately NOT attempted, and that is the same refusal `3ucw` recorded.** `chpeMetadataPointer` has never once been observed non-zero on any file here — measured at `11408ac`, `t64`/`w64` have no load-config directory at all, the ARM64 pair read 0, and the PE32 pair declare a structure too short to reach the field — so a consumer of it would be unverifiable in both directions. What a future reader needs is written down instead of half-built: `ExtraRFETable` is at **0x40** and `ExtraRFETableSize` at **0x44** of `IMAGE_ARM64EC_METADATA` (all-`ULONG` fields, `Version` first), both RVAs, and the schema is the **complement** of the machine word.
  - **The two hybrid machine constants are GONE, and one of them was WRONG rather than merely unreachable.** `isArm64Machine` sent `IMAGE_FILE_MACHINE_ARM64EC` (0xA641) and `ARM64X` (0xA64E) down the ARM64 path as a "deliberate superset", on the reasoning that it would be right if either ever reached a `coffHeader`. The table refutes half of that: 0xA641 means an ARM64EC image, whose exception directory is the **x64** table, so the arm would have read 12-byte entries at an 8-byte stride — the `peek-a-bin-kwc` desynchronisation `parsePdata`'s own docstring opens by warning about, arrived at from the other direction. 0xA64E's intention was right and is already served by 0xAA64, the word an ARM64X image actually carries; keeping an unreachable duplicate of a correct answer beside a refuted one only invites re-adding both. **Do not re-add either.**
  - **The deletion is provably output-neutral and is pinned by a test rather than by a gate**, since neither constant reaches a linked image: `npm run corpus` byte-identical, `corpus:arm64` 51/51. `pdata.test.ts`'s new cases use a well-formed **x64** fixture so they show what the withdrawn arm *would have done* — at an 8-byte stride those 24 bytes read as one packed entry claiming a 0x1054-byte function at 0x1000, fiction and longer than the whole table describes — and the negative control (restore the arm) fails exactly those two tests and nothing else.
  - **NOT VERIFIED, and it must be said as plainly as `3ucw` said it: this rests entirely on documentation.** No ARM64EC or ARM64X binary exists on this machine, so neither table has been seen, no `ExtraRFETable` value has been read, and the ARM64X fixup swap has never been observed. Two things are inferences rather than quotes even so: that a *static* reader of an ARM64X file sees the un-fixed-up ARM64 view follows from the relocations being *dynamic*, and the ARM64EC/ARM64X split of `pdata`/`hybridPdata` is read off lld's code rather than stated in prose by Microsoft.
- **…and where the image DECLARES CHPE metadata the refusal says so, instead of inferring hybrid-ness from the rate.** `PEFile.loadConfig.chpeMetadataPointer` (`peek-a-bin-7p5t`) is the format's only declaration of hybrid-ness, and it now reaches `Arm64DecodeRateError` through `App` → `disasmClient.configure` → `WorkerState` → `Arm64Context`. Six things (`peek-a-bin-3ucw`):
  - **It is consumed for PROSE ONLY, and that is what makes it safe rather than what makes it cheap.** `peek-a-bin-7p5t` deliberately left the field with no consumer because "a wrong CHPE reading that starts rejecting genuine ARM64 is worse than the current heuristic, and it cannot be tested here". That reason does not apply to a message: the throw condition, the `decoded`/`words` fields and every caller's behaviour are untouched, so the failure mode it declined to risk is **structurally impossible** here. Pinned by a test that refuses and accepts the identical section under all three states of the field.
  - **It is OPTIONAL, not a dependency.** An image with no load-config directory, a structure too short to reach the field, or a caller that was never threaded produces exactly the message this threw before. Three states, and only a **non-zero** value is evidence: `0` means present-and-not-hybrid, which says *not* hybrid, and `undefined` means unreadable — collapsing those two is what would make the field useless.
  - **It names ARM64X rather than "ARM64EC or ARM64X"**, which is sharper than `peek-a-bin-3ucw` anticipated and follows from the machine-type finding above: this sweep is only ever reached for an image marked ARM64, and a final ARM64EC image is marked x64.
  - **Session state on the worker, not a per-request field** — the opposite of `machine`, and for a stated reason: `peek-a-bin-x4o2`'s hazard is that session state cannot decide the architecture of a message posted before the `configure` that set it, and this decides nothing. A decode serviced early throws the pre-existing message. It is keyed on **`machine` being declared** rather than on its own presence, because a declared machine type is the load handshake: guarding on its own presence instead leaves one file's CHPE pointer attached to the next file's refusal, which is a test.
  - **Not in any cache key.** `Arm64SweepCache`'s answer is a function of the bytes, the load address and the decoder, so a fourth argument for a message must not be able to cause a miss — that would make one file's prose cost another file's sweep. Tested by identity of the returned array plus the Capstone call count.
  - **`mcp/disasm.ts` is deliberately NOT threaded**, and that is a proportionality judgement rather than an oversight: its three entry points are 7-to-10-parameter functions, the consumer there is a tool client rather than a person, and the field's own contract already says an untreaded caller gets the previous message. `mcp/session.ts` holds the `PEFile`, so the value is one field access away if that ever changes.

  **NOT VERIFIED against a real file, and this cannot be fixed here**: there is no ARM64EC or ARM64X binary on this machine, so the non-zero branch is fixture-only. Measured at `11408ac`, the only two images here that read the field at all are `t64-arm.exe` and `w64-arm.exe`, both reading **0** — so the hybrid sentence cannot fire on any file available to test — while `t64.exe` and `w64.exe` have **no load-config directory** and the two PE32 binaries declare 0x48 bytes where the 32-bit field needs 0x80. Six negative controls, each failing 1–3 tests and only its own: the throw dropping the argument, the cache keying on it, `0` treated as evidence, `armCtx` not forwarding it, `configure` guarding on its own presence, and the client dropping it from the message.
- **`.pdata` is authoritative for x64 function boundaries and beats prologue scanning.** Where a `.pdata` range exists, a prologue-byte or padding-heuristic candidate strictly inside it is a re-detection of a function already known exactly, not a new function: t64 went 511 → 279 functions, 232 overlapping pairs → 0, 93 fully-contained → 0, 53 empty decompiled bodies → 16. Evidence about an *entry point* — call target, jump-table target, export, entry point, unwind handler, `.pdata` begin — still wins inside a range. PE32 has no `.pdata` to arbitrate and still over-produces (`peek-a-bin-abv`).
- **An MSVC x86 `__finally` funclet is not a function, and the SEH scope table must not be used to PROTECT one.** The funclet is emitted *inside* its parent, reached by a `call` from it, ends in `ret`, and the parent resumes on the next byte — so it is a call target, hence a function start by every other rule, and since sizes are distance-to-next-start it cuts its parent in half and the parent loses every `jcc` aiming past the new end. `interiorBranchedOverStarts` (`functionDetect.ts`) withdraws such a start when nothing outside the previous function calls it *and* a conditional jump that function can actually execute crosses it; that is what took t32 from 293 to 288 functions at `95229d3`, folding five funclet bodies into the five parents, with the instruction stream byte-identical either way. The trap for later: **the file does name two of those five** — 0x4058A6 and 0x4063B8 are handler entries in their parents' scope tables in `.rdata` (`{EnclosingLevel -2, Filter NULL, Handler …}`, and a NULL filter means `__finally`) — so `strong` means "named by a table the parser reads", not "named by the file". Parsing that table and feeding its handlers into `strong` would re-break `sub_4031A4`, whose funclet the table names at 0x403270 with the withdrawn 0x403276 six bytes inside it. The table's real content is *funclet-of-parent*, which belongs in a relation, not in a set that protects a start (`peek-a-bin-sysf`, `peek-a-bin-gtlf`). **That relation has since landed — `src/disasm/seh32.ts` and the fifth admission, `peek-a-bin-d827` — and the warning above is now MEASURED rather than reasoned**: feeding the tables' named addresses into `strong` instead re-introduces **9 withdrawn starts on t32 and 7 on w32** at `f3b89ec`, 0x4058A6 and 0x4063B8 among them, each cutting its parent in half again. Two corrections to the sentence above, both checked against the bytes: the records are at 0x411228 and 0x4112B8 and it is their *handler field* that sits at 0x411230 and 0x4112C0, and the layout is a 16-byte header (`GSCookieOffset`, `GSCookieXOROffset`, `EHCookieOffset`, `EHCookieXOROffset`) before the first 12-byte record.
- **…and `interiorBranchedOverStarts` now has THREE admissions, not one, because a tail funclet is not branched over at all.** The rule above needs the parent to have code *past* the funclet, and most of MSVC's x86 funclets sit at the parent's **tail**, after its last `ret`. So 8 starts on t32 and 6 on w32 survived it (at `99203fb`, which is also where the function counts below were taken) — every one a `push dword ptr [ebp + N]; call; pop ecx; ret` or the 46-byte pair at t32 0x40a631 / w32 0x40921c — and each was emitted as a standalone function reading `*(int32_t*)(ebp + 8)` with **nothing in it assigning `ebp`**. The third admission is a property of the candidate rather than of its surroundings: **`[ebp + N]` in the FIRST instruction at an address is a read of a frame some other function established**, because nothing has run there yet to set one up and no x86 calling convention passes EBP. Withdrawn, the same read is `arg_0` — the parent's own slot, nameable because the frame is now the frame of the function the read sits in, and `t32!sub_401DB3` overwrites `[ebp+8]` at 0x401e05 so the funclet reads the *updated* argument. Four things (`peek-a-bin-qe8z`):
  - **At least one caller is REQUIRED, and it is the whole soundness argument.** The second admission (below) is about a start nothing reaches; this one is the opposite.
