It also would have meant editing `pe/parser.ts`, which a sibling owned that session; the derivation needed nothing there. **`dataDirectoryClamp` (`src/pe/dataDirectories.ts`) is the one declaration**, on `pe/sections.ts`'s model: two readers in different top-level directories, and a predicate hand-written at two sites is this repo's most frequently repaired defect. It returns `null` for a whole table rather than a `{ clamped: false }` shape, so a caller cannot render an admission for a file that earned none. It carries a **`reason`** — `"spec-maximum"` when the file declared more than the sixteen the format defines, `"short-header"` when the declared entries do not fit in the file at all — because those are different findings and only the second says bytes are missing; `short-header` wins when both are true, since "the file ends mid-table" says more than "the count is out of range". Both arms are reachable and both are tested: the second by slicing a fixture buffer, which `parsePE` survives. The anomaly is **`warning`**: `critical` in that pass is reserved for WX sections and an entry point in writable memory, and nothing here says code will run — but no toolchain produces this by accident either, so not `info`. **No static instrument can see any of it**: both numbers are `number`, the corpus never renders, and `npm run corpus` is not applicable to a change confined to a view and the anomaly detector. The instruments are `HeaderView.dom.test.tsx` and `analysis/__tests__/anomalies.test.ts`, both asserting against the fixture's own bytes. Eight negative controls were run and **all eight discriminated, none inert**: the admission deleted (three rows red); the predicate widened to `present > declared` so it fires on a whole table (the two liveness rows red, plus a pre-existing row that already pinned the exact string `"16"`); the anomaly push deleted; the `reason` pinned to one value; and the table rendered at a different length. The two halves of the mismatch are separate `it`s deliberately — in one `it` the first failure hides the second, and "the parenthetical is wrong" and "the table is a different length than it claims" are different regressions. (`peek-a-bin-dd94`)

- **A derived label must be derived from the value it labels, and a flag row must admit the bits its table cannot name.** Two further `HeaderView` findings, both filed by an earlier pass and both taken here. (a) **`Magic`** spelled its parenthetical `pe.is64 ? "PE32+" : "PE32"` — and `is64` **is** `magic === 0x020B`, so the label was not derived from the magic at all: every third value would have printed `(PE32)`, including 0x0107, a ROM image, which is neither. That is the `ImageBase` class again — a derived spelling that is right for the values anyone has looked at and states something false for the rest — and it is exactly what the Machine and Subsystem rows on either side refuse by admitting `(Unknown)`. **It is UNREACHABLE through `parsePE` today**, which throws on any magic but 0x010B and 0x020B, so the change is a guard against that widening rather than a repair of something on screen; saying so is the point, and the test builds the state by hand and says it cannot come from the parser. (b) **`COFF_CHARACTERISTICS` was missing all three deprecated bits** — 0x0010 AGGRESSIVE_WS_TRIM, 0x0080 BYTES_REVERSED_LO, 0x8000 BYTES_REVERSED_HI — and, worse, **neither flag row said anything at all about a set bit it could not name**, so `0x0042` rendered exactly the chips `0x0002` would. Deprecated is not unnamed: "the OS ignores this bit" is a different answer from silence. The admission cannot be closed by completing the table instead, because **0x0040 is reserved by the format and has no name to give it** — so `decodeFlags` returns the leftover mask beside the names and `FlagChips` prints `(unknown bits: 0x0040)`. Three details: the admission is deliberately **not a chip**, since a chip is this panel's spelling for "the format names this bit and the file set it", which is the opposite of what it says, and because the suite's `chips()` helper reads `span.rounded` and an admission wearing a chip's clothes would start appearing in every flag-list assertion; `FlagChips`' `flags.length === 0` early return had to learn about the mask, or a word whose only set bits are unnamed reports as `none` (both directions are tested); and the `>>> 0` on the mask is **defensive and currently inert**, every flag word here being a `uint16` so bit 31 can never be set — recorded rather than presented as load-bearing, the same operator having been a real defect two entries above. (`peek-a-bin-dd94`)

- **`lineMap` is MANY-TO-ONE, so anything keying a rendered element off an address alone renders one per sharing line.** Several emitted C lines routinely carry one instruction address, and two other places in the tree already model it that way: `DisassemblyView` builds an address → `line[]` map to feed `highlightLines`, and `emit.ts`'s `placeGotoLabels` says in as many words that "if an address somehow appears twice the earlier copy is the one the jump was structured around". `DecompileView` decided `isEditing` per line from `editingComment.address` alone, so the `;` shortcut — which sets `editingComment` for exactly ONE address — mounted a `<textarea>` on every line sharing it: N identical edit boxes for one comment, each running `focusOnMount`, focus landing on the last of them. **The fix is a `useMemo` resolving the address to one anchor line and taking the LOWEST** — `placeGotoLabels`' own tiebreak, and the line the auto-scroll effect brings into view with `Math.min`, so the editor now opens on the line the panel just scrolled to. Two details are load-bearing: `syncDisabled` is re-tested inside the memo rather than inherited from `lineAddr`, because on the AI tab the map numbers a different body and that is what the pre-fix expression got right by accident; and the `!= null` in the new `isEditing` narrows `editingComment` for the editor's JSX exactly as the expression it replaces did. **Nothing static could see it** — both spellings compile and type-check, and nothing under `corpus/` renders React. Restoring the per-line test reddens the pin "opens exactly ONE editor when several lines share the edited address", re-derived by the integrator. (`peek-a-bin-p0qw`)

- **A NUL-terminated string read out of a PE must be bounded, and the bound cannot just be the record's own declared size — `parseDebugDirectory`'s PDB path scan is where both halves of that bit.** `parseDebugDirectory` (`pe/metadata.ts`) read the CodeView RSDS GUID and age at fixed offsets and then scanned for the PDB path's terminating NUL from `pointerToRawData + 24` **forward through the whole buffer**, decoding everything it passed. `HeaderView` calls it inside a `useMemo`, i.e. **during a render on the main thread**, and this tool is expected to open images of a couple of hundred MiB, so the bad case is a multi-hundred-MiB `TextDecoder` in a render rather than a slow parse in a worker. It could never read outside the `ArrayBuffer`, so this is a **denial of service and a wrong-value question, not memory safety**. **Measured** at `82e2f60` on a 1,050,112-byte fixture whose bytes past the record are NUL-free: the old scan returned a `pdbPath` of **1,049,036 characters** with nothing marking it, against **4,109** after the fix; the shape is `fileLength - pathStart`, so at the ~253 MiB ceiling the old number is ~265,000,000. **The control that exposed it was inert, and the inertness was the measurement** — zeroing a debug entry's `SizeOfData` moved no row in `HeaderView`'s suite, because nothing read the field at all (`peek-a-bin-nygv`).
  - **The bound is `min(declared size, absolute cap, end of buffer)`, and each of the three is there because the other two are insufficient.** `SizeOfData` alone is refused because it is **as attacker-controlled as the bytes it describes and is the only bound that can be too SMALL**: an understated value shortens a path that is well-formed on disk, trading a hang for a wrong answer — and the wrong answer is the worse of the two here, because the path is read *out* of the tool (a symbol server wants `<name>.pdb/<GUID><Age>/<name>.pdb`), so a short one matches nothing while looking entirely well-formed doing it. That is the `Ordinal_<n>`/imphash trap in a second place. A cap alone is refused because a cap generous enough never to truncate a real path still lets a hostile record spend the whole cap, and because it throws away the one statement the file makes about its own record. `MAX_PDB_PATH_BYTES` is **4096**: far above anything `link.exe` emits (an ANSI build-machine path, historically capped at `MAX_PATH` = 260) and far below anything whose decode costs a render.
  - **A declared size too small to hold the fixed part plus a terminator is not honoured, and that is a rule about CREDIBILITY rather than a special case for zero.** `SizeOfData <= 24` says nothing about the path, so it drops to the cap. Two reasons: honouring it would silently truncate a valid path on a file whose only fault is an unfilled field, which is the exact correctness defect this whole entry is about; and the fixed part is read *above* without consulting `SizeOfData` at all, so a record declaring less than it visibly contains has already been read past and honouring a zero here would be inconsistent as well as useless. Measured: dropping this test alone reddens the "SizeOfData is zero" row.
  - **THE ADMISSION IS SPELLED INTO THE VALUE, not only into the type, and that half is load-bearing.** The doctrine is that a narrower answer must not wear a complete one's shape — `DetectResult.omitted` names the passes that did not run, `analysisNotice` states a diagnosis, `emit.ts` prints `__unrecovered_N` rather than something plausible. A truncated path rendered as a path is precisely that failure. So a cut-short scan returns the prefix followed by `PDB_PATH_TRUNCATION_MARKER` (`… <truncated>`, containing characters no Windows path may hold) **and** sets `DebugInfo.pdbPathTruncated`. The flag is the machine-readable half; the marker is the half that reaches a reader, and it is not redundant with it: **the one render site prints `d.pdbPath` verbatim and knows nothing about the flag**, the app has no toast mechanism, and one must not be invented for a bug fix (the `copyText` refusal). A design that set `pdbPath` only when complete was **considered and refused** for the same reason — `HeaderView` renders the GUID row whenever `guid` is set, so withholding the path would have printed a debug entry with no indication that a path was there and could not be read.
  - **Six negative controls, every one discriminating, none inert.** Run against `src/pe/__tests__/malformed.test.ts` at `82e2f60`: remove the cap → 2 red (the two no-NUL rows); remove the declared-size bound → 1 red (the understating row); make the LARGER bound win instead of the smaller → 3 red; drop the marker and the flag → 4 red (every truncation row); honour `SizeOfData` unconditionally → 2 red; restore the pre-fix scan verbatim → 4 red, which is the row that says the suite would have caught the original defect. **One honest note about an instrument**: the "SizeOfData is zero" row is *still* inert as a before/after control — it is green before the fix and green after it, by two different routes — because the field being unread and the field being judged incredible produce the same answer. It is not useless (it is the only guard against honouring the field unconditionally), but the row that proves the field is read at all is the understating one.
  - **`npm run corpus` is byte-identical over all four binaries** (260/279/258/275 functions, 1072 in total, at `82e2f60`), which here is a statement that the change stayed inside the path it meant to touch — the PE parser is in the corpus import graph and is checked differentially against an independently written from-spec reader, but nothing under `corpus/` calls `parseDebugDirectory`.
  - **Two neighbours were reported here and BOTH have since moved; this bullet is kept because the reasoning is still the record of why they were left.** `readCString` (`pe/parser.ts`) — the import/export name reader — had a `maxLength = 1024` cap since before this and truncated **silently**, which is the same shape this entry argues against; it now carries `NAME_TRUNCATION_MARKER`, see the next entry. `readWString` (`pe/resources.ts`) is bounded by the resource's own declared extent and is unchanged. The remaining walk bounded only by end-of-file, the import thunk loop, is the next entry's subject.

- **…and where the truncated thing is a LIST rather than a string, the admission goes on the COUNT and the digest refuses — `parseImports`' two walks are the case that settles it.** The thunk walk in `parseImports` (`pe/parser.ts`) stopped at a zero thunk and was otherwise bounded **only by the end of the file**, so an unterminated thunk array pushed one import entry per pointer-width slot to EOF, inside `parsePE`, on the main thread, before anything renders. Same family as `nygv` — attacker-controlled extent, unbounded scan, main-thread cost — and deliberately a different fix. **Measured at `5baec33`** on a 1 MiB fixture: 262,080 functions in one library, against `MAX_IMPORT_FUNCTIONS` (65,536) after; the shape is `sectionBytes / thunkSize`, so on the ~253 MiB image this tool expects to open the old number is tens of millions. The descriptor walk above it was already bounded by the directory's declared size, but that field is a uint32 the file supplies: declared `0xFFFFFFFF` it yielded **52,416 libraries**, each with its own name decode (`peek-a-bin-tmo9`).
  - **THE COST THAT MATTERS IS THE PRODUCT, WHICH IS WHY THE FUNCTION BUDGET IS GLOBAL AND NOT PER-LIBRARY.** Every descriptor may name the same unterminated thunk array, so capping each walk separately still multiplies out. **Measured, not reasoned**: with 52,416 descriptors all naming one unterminated array, the pre-fix parser did not merely hang — node died with `FATAL ERROR: Ineffective mark-compacts near heap limit`, i.e. **~4 GB of import entries out of a ONE MEGABYTE file**. In a browser that is the tab. The same perturbation applied to the landed code (control A below) still kills the vitest worker, so that row's protection is demonstrated by a *dead process* rather than by a normal red assertion — worth knowing before reading its control output.
  - **The bounds are `min(containing section, declared size, count cap, buffer)`, four ways, smallest winning, on `nygv`'s model.** The **section** bound (`sectionRawLimitForRva`) is the one that improves the ANSWER rather than only the cost: an import array the file places inside a section does not continue past it, so without the bound a missing terminator at the end of `.rdata` reads the next section's bytes as thunks — measured at **192 → 4,288** entries under control C, the whole of an adjacent `.data`. It is also evidence the file itself provides and cannot inflate beyond the image, which is exactly `SizeOfData`'s role in `nygv`, except that it can never be too small: an array crossing a section boundary is already malformed. It does **not** bound the work (a crafted file may declare a 200 MiB section), so the **count caps** are what stop the hostile case and neither replaces the other. Section selection was factored to **one declaration** (`scanSectionForRva` / `sectionForRva` / `offsetInSection`) rather than restated for the limit, and `rvaToFileOffset` and `rvaToFileOffsetIndexed` now both answer from it.
  - **A LIST CANNOT CARRY A MARKER, AND INVENTING AN ENTRY WOULD BE WORSE THAN SILENCE.** `nygv` spelled its truncation into the value because the value was a string and the render site printed it verbatim. A `<truncated>` element in an import list is a **lie inside a list that feeds `computeImphash`, the Imports tab, the IAT map, xref building and the MCP tools** — the `Ordinal_<n>` trap with a new spelling. So the admission takes the three forms a list can carry it in: `ImportEntry.truncated` and `PEFile.importsTruncated` (the machine-readable half, on `ResourceTree.truncated`'s model); the Imports tab's own **counts**, since the count is the sentence a reader actually reads about a list's extent; and a **refusal from `computeImphash`**.
  - **`computeImphash` returns `null` rather than a digest, and that is the decision the whole change turns on.** An imphash over a short list is not a degraded answer — it is a well-formed answer to a different question: a hex string indistinguishable from a correct one, never validated against anything inside this tool, whose only use is comparison with another tool's output. It therefore **fails by matching nothing while looking entirely correct**. There is no marker to spell into it, because any string it returns is either pefile's answer or a lie, so silence labelled by the type is the only honest narrowing. **Its parameter changed from `PEFile["imports"]` to the `PEFile`** for that reason and not for tidiness: whether the LIST is whole is a fact about the parse and not about any element, so a caller holding only the array cannot establish it, and taking the file makes forgetting to ask a **compile error** instead of a silently wrong hash. `""` still means "imports nothing"; `HeaderView` prints a different sentence for each, because collapsing them would print "No imports" over a table that was merely cut short.
  - **`readCString`'s silent truncation is fixed with `nygv`'s marker, but the marker ALONE would be wrong here and that asymmetry is the finding.** A 1024-byte cap has been on it since long before this; measured at `5baec33`, a crafted `.rdata` produced a library name of **exactly 1024 characters, tail `"AAAAAAAA"`, with nothing marking it** — a name that reads as the file's own. A PDB path is read out of the tool by a human; these names are also read by `computeImphash`, `matchesApi`, `resolveOrdinal` and the IAT map, so a marker there **changes a digest** rather than merely labelling a value. The resolution is that a marked name additionally makes the **entry** not whole, which makes the **table** not whole, which makes the hash refuse — so a marked name can never reach a digest. Truncation is decided **exactly** ("the read stopped at the bound *and* no NUL sits at it"), or a name of exactly 1024 bytes plus its terminator would be reported cut short and the imphash withheld from a file with nothing wrong with it.
  - **The pre-existing forwarder row had pinned the silent truncation AS THE RULE** (`expect(forwarder).toMatch(/^A+$/)` — a clipped forwarder asserted to read exactly like a complete one) and went red the moment `readCString` began marking. That is the shape this repo keeps finding, and it is worth its own note: `parseForwarder` splits a forwarder on its dot to name a DLL and a symbol, so a silently clipped one resolves to a target the file never named.
  - **Nine negative controls, every one discriminating, none inert.** Against `malformed.test.ts` + the two `.dom` suites at `5baec33`: remove the global function budget → 1 red **and the worker dies of OOM**; remove the descriptor cap → 2 red (52,416 libraries); remove the section bound → 1 red (192 → 4,288); make the LARGER bound win → **5 red**, three of them `RangeError: Offset is outside the bounds of the DataView`; keep the bounds but drop the admission → 6 red; let `computeImphash` hash a truncated table → 3 red; restore `readCString`'s silent truncation → 2 red; decide truncation on "reached the bound" instead of "no NUL at the bound" → 1 red (the exactly-at-the-cap row); render no admission in the Imports tab → 2 red.
  - **`npm run corpus` is byte-identical over all four binaries** (260/279/258/275, 1072 functions, base and change both pinned to `5baec33`), and the six real binaries are unchanged in every import count with `importsTruncated` undefined and no marker anywhere — which for a parser change is the case rather than a formality, since a cap that truncates a real file has traded a hang for a wrong answer.
  - **`ResourceTree.truncated` is the precedent and it was only HALF built.** `parseResourceDirectory` had set that flag since before this and **no view had ever rendered it**, so a resource tree cut short by `MAX_TOTAL_ENTRIES` read on screen exactly like a complete one. Not touched here (a sibling owned that file); landed as the next entry.

- **…and the resource tree's half of it, where the admission goes on the COUNT LINE rather than on a row, plus the three places the flag was not being set at all — a hole the FIELD'S NAME caused.** `parseResourceDirectory` (`pe/resources.ts`) had carried `ResourceTree.truncated` since long before `tmo9` and nothing rendered it. The reader-facing half is now two things in `ResourcesView.tsx`, and they are separate because the view has two exits that mislead differently (`peek-a-bin-dhcx`).
  - **THE COUNT LINE, and this is the half where the choice differs from `ImportsView`'s.** The heading is `Resources (N types, M entries)` — a sentence about the list's extent, which is `tmo9`'s own finding — so a short tree makes it describe a smaller file entirely plausibly. The admission sits **beside the counts and not on any row**, because the budget is **global to the walk**: a single `Budget` object is threaded by reference through every `walkDirectory` frame, so when the allowance runs out the walk breaks out of whichever directory it had reached and *every ancestor above it is equally short*. There is no row that is "the incomplete one", and marking one would state a fact the flag does not carry. `ImportEntry.truncated` is per-library precisely because each descriptor has its own thunk walk — the asymmetry is a property of the two budgets, not a style difference. The counts are **qualified rather than replaced**: what survived is as true as it ever was, so withholding it would trade one wrong shape for another (`XrefPanel`'s fall-back-visibly rule).
  - **THE EMPTY ARM IS THE WORSE HALF.** `entries.length === 0` took the same exit as a PE with no resource directory and printed **"No resources found in this PE file."** — a positive claim about the *file*, which a cut-short walk makes false rather than merely narrow. It is `HeaderView`'s imphash distinction in view form (`""` means "imports nothing", `null` means "the table is not whole"; collapsing them prints "No imports" over a table that was merely cut short). **Reachable with no leaf at all**, and that is not a contrivance: the allowance is spent on *directory* entries, so a root declaring more subdirectories than the budget exhausts it before `walkDirectory` ever descends to a data entry. The calm sentence stays for a genuinely empty directory — a directory with nothing in it and no directory at all really are one fact to a reader; only a cut-short walk is a different one.
  - **THE FIELD WAS CALLED `stopped`, AND THAT NAME COST THREE SITES.** `ResourceTree.truncated` means "an entry the file declares was not walked" — its own docstring said so — but `stopped` reads as a fact about the *budget*, so three other places where the walk abandons declared entries each broke out or returned early without touching it, and the tree they produced was a short one wearing a complete one's shape: (1) `entryOffset + 8 > view.byteLength`, the buffer ending inside an entry array whose length is the file's own two uint16 counts; (2) `absOffset + 16 > view.byteLength`, a subdirectory the parent declares whose header the buffer does not hold; (3) `rvaToFileOffset(...) < 0` in `parseResourceDirectory`, which returned `{ root: [], entries: [] }` — byte-for-byte the answer a PE with no resource directory gets, for a file `parsePE` only calls the walk on *because* its data directory declares a non-zero RVA and size. The field is `incomplete` now and every one of the three sets it.
  - **A PRE-EXISTING TEST HAD PINNED THE SILENT NARROWING AS THE RULE**, the `tmo9` forwarder row's shape exactly: `expect(tree).toEqual({ root: [], entries: [] })` for the unmapped-RVA case, asserting that a declared-but-unresolvable resource directory reads identically to a file with none. It went red the moment the third site began admitting, and is now written the other way round with the reasoning attached.
  - **TWO EARLY RETURNS DELIBERATELY DO NOT SET IT, and they are marked at their sites.** `depth >= MAX_DEPTH` and `visited.has(dirOffset)` are judgements about a **malformed shape** rather than about an unread entry — a PE resource directory is three levels by construction, so the depth limit is only reached by a file nesting further than the format allows, and a repeat visit is a cycle. The second has a real cost (a subdirectory legitimately shared by two referrers is emptied for the second) and is filed rather than swept in, because calling it "the walk was cut short" is arguable in a way the three above are not.
  - **Eleven negative controls, every one discriminating, none inert**, over a baseline of 90 rows across the two suites. Drop the count-line admission → 2 red; drop the empty-arm admission → 1 red; render both admissions unconditionally → **6 red**, three of them *pre-existing* rows, which is what says the calm sentence was already pinned; stop recording the budget stop in the parser → 5 red across both suites; one control per new site (entry array → 1, subdirectory header → 1, unmapped RVA → 1); set `incomplete` unconditionally → 9 red; `readResourceString` marks nothing → 2 red; `readWString` marks nothing → 1 red; and decide either marker on **reaching** the bound rather than on falling short of the declared count → 2 red, one in each reader — that last discriminates for the version reader only because a terminated exactly-4096-character row was built for it, and without that row the control would have been inert there. The fixture-liveness row is asserted *before* the component in every case, because every view row is vacuous if the walk is not actually truncated or if the surviving leaves do not survive.
  - **…AND THE TWO RESOURCE STRING CLIPS TAKE `nygv`'S MARKER, WITH THE ASYMMETRY AGAINST `readCString` STATED RATHER THAN LEFT AS AN ACCIDENT.** `readResourceString` clipped a resource name at `MAX_RESOURCE_STRING` (4096) and `parseVersionInfo`'s `readWString` stopped collecting a key or value at the same bound, both **silently**, where `parser.ts`'s import/export name reader now appends `NAME_TRUNCATION_MARKER`. Both values are printed verbatim by a render site — a resource id through `ordinalLabel`, a version string straight into `ExpandedLeaf`'s table — so they are `nygv`'s case exactly, and `RESOURCE_STRING_TRUNCATION_MARKER` is the repair. **The asymmetry is that nothing here feeds a digest**: `readCString`'s marker had to be accompanied by marking the *entry* not-whole, so that a marked name could never reach `computeImphash` and change a hash; a resource name and a version string reach no hash, no comparison and no cross-tool oracle, so the marker alone is the whole fix and no refusal accompanies it. **Both are decided EXACTLY**, which is the `peek-a-bin-6qx9` lesson applied one reader over: `readResourceString` tests what it collected against the character count the string's *own uint16 header* declares — one test covering the cap and the buffer ending mid-string, since falling short of the declared count is the truncation whichever caused it — and `readWString` records `dropped` at the drop site rather than inferring it from `chars.length` afterwards, because a properly terminated value of exactly 4096 characters is a complete read. **Two pre-existing rows had pinned both silences as the rule**: `expect((tree.root[0].id as string).length).toBeLessThanOrEqual(4096)`, true of a clipped name and of the file's own alike, and `expect(info.Key).toMatch(/^A+$/)` — the `tmo9` forwarder row's shape verbatim. Both went red on the marker and are now written the other way round. **A third copy of the literal `… <truncated>` now exists** (`metadata.ts`, `parser.ts`, `resources.ts`), following the one-constant-per-reader pattern the first two established; folding them into one declaration is the right end state and is filed rather than done, because it touches two files this change does not own.
  - **None of the three new sites can fire on a well-formed image** — each needs the file to declare something the buffer does not hold — and `npm run corpus:parserdiff` is the control: **98/118 green, 20 vacuous, 0 red**, with its `resources: walk truncated by the entry budget` row at 0 on **all six** real binaries. For a parser change that is the case rather than a formality, since a flag that fires on a real file has traded a silence for a false warning. `npm run corpus` names four binaries, 27/27, unmoved.
  - **`ResourceTree.truncated` is the precedent and it is only HALF built.** `parseResourceDirectory` has set that flag since before this and **no view has ever rendered it**, so a resource tree cut short by `MAX_TOTAL_ENTRIES` reads on screen exactly like a complete one. Not touched here (a sibling owns that file), and reported rather than fixed.
- **THE DELIBERATE SWEEP: every reader in `src/pe/` was audited for an attacker-controlled bound, and SEVEN more sites were open — one of them a wrong value rather than a slow one.** `nygv` and `tmo9` were both found *incidentally, while doing something else*, two days apart, and the second one killed node (`FATAL ERROR: Ineffective mark-compacts near heap limit`, ~4 GB of import entries out of a one-megabyte file). Nobody had ever swept for the class on purpose. This entry is what that sweep found. Every reader was asked the five questions the class turns on: what bounds it; whether the *work* is bounded or only the reads; whether two file-supplied counts **multiply**; whether anything allocates a size the file chose; and whether it runs on the main thread. **The instrument is a returned value or count and never a timing** — several agents share this machine — and every figure below was taken at `d8d8a6d` by running the identical fixture against the pre-fix reader. Ranked by severity:
  - **1. `parseArm64Pdata`'s unwind-code walk — the worst by wall clock, and a genuine PRODUCT.** An `.xdata` record's extension word gives `codeWords` eight bits, so one record may carry 255 * 4 = **1020** code bytes, which `parseArm64XdataRecord` copied into a fresh `number[]` and then walked; nothing stopped **every** entry in the table from naming the **same** record, and `count` was `Math.floor(size / 8)` over a `uint32` with the buffer as its only backstop. Measured with `size = 0xFFFFFFFF`: a **69,632-byte** file walked **8,225,280** unwind bytes in **8.3 s**, a **266,240-byte** one **33,292,800** in **37.7 s** — linear in file size, so the ~253 MiB images this tool opens are *hours*, in `parsePE`, on the main thread. A 1 MiB fixture did not finish inside a two-minute harness. **The fix is one budget for the whole table** (`MAX_ARM64_UNWIND_CODE_BYTES`), because `tmo9` already settled that capping each of two file-supplied counts still multiplies out; 4 MiB is calibrated against the real tables rather than picked round (`t64-arm`: 419 entries, 156 `.xdata` records, **1220 code bytes in total**, **28** in the largest single record; `w64-arm`: 381 / 144 / 1100 / 28 — so the format's 1020-byte ceiling is ~36x anything a linker emits and 4 MiB is ~3400x either whole-table total, while still covering an ntoskrnl-scale 100k-entry table at the observed 28 bytes each). After: **both sizes walk 4,194,240 bytes in ~4.3 s, and the cost stops growing with the file** — the flatness is the claim, not the digits. The per-record `number[]` is now a `Uint8Array` window (`frameFromUnwindCodes` takes `ArrayLike<number>`, which a walk that only indexes and reads `.length` cannot tell apart), worth 25 s → 18 s on the 16 MiB case on its own. **What the budget cannot fix, and it is worth knowing:** `readUnwindCode` allocates two closures and a result object *per byte*, so the walk costs ~1.1 µs a byte and the 4 MiB ceiling is still ~4 s — of which a legitimate 100k-entry ARM64 image already pays ~3 s today. That is a property of the decoder, not of the bound, and rewriting it does not belong in a bounds change.
  - **2. `parseDebugDirectory`'s entry count — `nygv`'s own defect, surviving with a different multiplier.** `nygv` bounded the per-record PDB path scan and left `numEntries = Math.floor(debugDir.size / 28)` bounded only by the buffer, so the **product** stayed open: every entry may name the same CodeView record and spend the whole of `MAX_PDB_PATH_BYTES`. Measured: a **1,065,472-byte** file whose debug directory declares `0xFFFFFFFF` and whose every 28-byte slot is a CodeView entry pointing at one unterminated record returned **38,034 entries carrying 153,877,941 characters of PDB path** — a 144x amplification, and `HeaderView` calls this inside a `useMemo`, i.e. **during a render**. After: 256 entries, 1,051,904 characters, 521 ms → 29 ms. `nygv`'s own test could not see it because it built one entry. `MAX_DEBUG_DIRECTORY_ENTRIES` is 256 against a real maximum of **one** (the four x86 corpus binaries) and **three** (the two ARM64 ones), and `DEBUG_TYPE_NAMES` knows 17 types in total, so a linker has nothing like 256 distinct records to emit; with the per-record cap the whole walk is bounded at 1 MiB of decoding.
  - **3. `parseExports` — three attacker-controlled counts, and the buffer was the only bound on any of them.** The suspicion going in was that `NumberOfNames` and `NumberOfFunctions` were a multiplying pair. **They are not**, and the reason is worth recording: the two walks are sequential, and the alias loop (`for (const name of names)`) is bounded by the *total* number of names because `namesByIndex` partitions them. What was there instead is a plain hundredfold amplification: a **1,049,088-byte** fixture declaring `0xFFFFFFFF` for both counts, with a name-pointer table of slots all aimed at one unterminated run, yielded **524,093 export entries carrying 104,696,157 characters of name** in `parsePE` on the main thread. After: 131,069 entries, 26,137,724 characters. Bounded four ways on `tmo9`'s model, and **`MAX_EXPORT_ENTRIES` is not an invented number** — the export ordinal table is an array of `uint16` holding the unbiased index into the address table, and the loader's ordinal space is a `uint16`, so 65536 is the *format's own* ceiling and a well-formed file cannot be cut short by it. That matters more here than anywhere else in this census, because **no binary on this machine exports anything**: `corpus:parserdiff`'s two export gates are VACUOUS, so the format argument is the whole of the evidence that the cap is above a real table (`ntdll.dll`'s ~2400 exports is documentation, not measurement). The admission is `PEFile.exportsTruncated` plus the Exports tab's count, on `importsTruncated`'s model; there is no export-side digest to refuse, which is why it needs no third form. The residual 26 MB of names is what a *legitimately maximal* export table costs and is the same exposure the import path already accepts.
  - **4. `parseImports`' parallel arrays desynchronised — A WRONG VALUE, not an omission, and the only row here that is not about cost.** `iatAddresses.push` was unconditional while `functions.push` happened only if the thunk's hint/name record resolved, so **one** unresolvable name RVA shifted every later name up a slot. Four consumers pair those arrays **by index** — `disasm/operands.ts`'s `buildIATLookup` (which is what labels a call site in the disassembly), `ImportsView`, `useVulnScanner`, `mcp/resources.ts` — so every remaining call site in that library was **labelled with another import's name, on real instructions**. No flag repairs that. A slot now contributes to **both** arrays or to **neither**, with `funcIndex` still counting every thunk so the surviving IAT VAs stay the addresses the loader will use; inventing a placeholder name to keep the lengths equal was the other option and is the `Ordinal_<n>` trap, since it puts a fabricated symbol into a list feeding `computeImphash` and `matchesApi`. **The structural fix is one array of `{ name, iatAddress }` pairs, so the desync is unrepresentable** — not taken, because it changes a `PEFile` shape read by four modules in three directories, and the invariant this loop now keeps is testable directly.
  - **5. Two more `tmo9` bypasses in `parseImports`, both silently narrowing.** An unresolvable *descriptor* name RVA `continue`d, so a whole imported library vanished with no flag — a shorter Imports tab reading as complete and a **confident imphash over a list missing a DLL**, which is precisely what `computeImphash`'s refusal exists to prevent. And an unresolvable *thunk array* yielded `KERNEL32.dll (0)`, indistinguishable from a library that genuinely imports nothing, which is a statement about the file. Both are one line and both route into machinery that already renders. `!thunkRVA` — no thunk array declared at all — is left unmarked deliberately.
  - **6. `parseBaseRelocations` — the declared size bounded the block walk and was then ignored by the entry walk inside it.** `entryCount` came from the block's own `SizeOfBlock` with the end of the *file* as its only backstop, so a directory declaring **eight bytes** — one block header — whose `SizeOfBlock` read `0xFFFFFFFF` produced **524,284 entries** out of a 1,049,088-byte fixture. **The pre-existing test asserted only `< buf.byteLength`, which 524,284 satisfies** — a bound with a test that could not see it. `(sizeOfBlock - 8) / 2` was also unfloored, so an odd block ran one iteration into its own half-entry. **What is deliberately NOT capped is the entry total**: a relocation table is legitimately O(image) — one block per 4 KiB page, up to 2044 entries each — so a 253 MiB image really does carry millions, there is no count above which a table is provably bogus, and the containing section's raw extent *is* the file's own statement about how far the array goes.
  - **7. `extractStrings` — the largest amplification in the census, and the only one off the main thread.** The per-section 1 MiB ceiling bounded each scan and nothing bounded its **product** with the section count, which is a `uint16` clamped only by `buffer / 40`; every section named `.rdata` is scanned and `pointerToRawData` may be 0 for all of them, so N sections each scan the same megabyte. Measured: 1600 such sections over an **883,712-byte** file produced **3,308,739 strings in 14.3 s**, and 6400 sections over 3,533,312 bytes **13,268,566 in 87.7 s** — linear in the section count, so a 1 MiB file declaring its buffer-full ~26,000 sections asks for ~26 GiB of scanning. After: **83,559 in 0.39 s and 95,030 in 0.43 s**, i.e. 36x and 204x, and flat where it was linear. One `MAX_STRING_SCAN_BYTES` budget for the whole call, shared by all four passes — a per-pass budget would multiply by four. Second tier only because this is the disasm RPC whose argument is the whole image, so the cost is a dead worker and an unbounded `Map` crossing `postMessage` rather than a frozen render.
  - **8. `readDERChildren` sized an array from the file.** A DER header is at least two bytes, so a container of nothing but two-byte elements yields `contentLen / 2` children, and `contentLen` is bounded only by `WIN_CERTIFICATE.dwLength`, which is bounded only by the buffer. Measured: a 1,048,576-byte file whose security directory covers all of it allocated **41.6 MB of heap in one call**, in `parsePE`, on the main thread; on a 253 MiB image that is ~10 GB, and `parseSecurityDirectory`'s `try/catch` **cannot catch an OOM**. After: 0.4 MB. Note what saved the *nesting* from also multiplying: DER children **partition** their parent's content range, so `extractCN`'s three levels sum to the top container's length rather than cubing it — but nothing bounded any single level. `MAX_DER_CHILDREN` is 4096 against single digits at every level X.509 and PKCS#7 actually use, and it is the **weakest-evidenced bound here**: no binary on this machine is signed, so `corpus:parserdiff` has nothing to say and the cap rests on the format and on documentation. A cut-short list needs no marker — every caller reads a *fixed field* by index and falls back to `subject: null` / `issuer: null`, which already means "not read" rather than "absent".
  - **`sectionRawLimitForRva` is now exported as the ONE declaration of the section bound**, because five walks outside `parseImports` wanted it and every one of them had the end of the file instead. It is the bound that improves the **answer** and not merely the cost — an array the file places inside a section does not continue past it — and it is evidence the file itself provides and cannot inflate beyond the image.
  - **Sites read and found sound, since a census that only lists hits says nothing about coverage:** `parseDataDirectories` (capped at the spec's 16), `parseSectionHeaders` (`uint16`, clamped to `buffer / 40`), `parseTLSDirectory`'s callback walk (a hard 256), `parseLoadConfig` (three claims, smallest winning, from `peek-a-bin-7p5t`), `parseRichHeader` (both scans confined to `[0x80, 0x400)`, so ~112 entries maximum — and it, too, runs in `HeaderView`'s render), `checksumFile` and `md5` (O(file) and O(the capped import list), both irreducible), `detectOverlay`, `parseArm64XdataRecord`'s `epilogCount` (arithmetic only, never a loop) and all of `arm64Unwind.ts` (every `readUnwindCode` width is ≥ 1, so the walk cannot stall, over an input the caller bounds).
  - **One site is sound only by a `uint16`, and it was checked rather than assumed.** `extractStrings` computes `imageEnd` with `Math.max(...sections.map(...))`. Measured on this V8: **65535 arguments is accepted and 125000 throws `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`** — and `numberOfSections` is a `uint16`, so the reachable maximum is exactly 65535. It is safe with nothing to spare, so the reason is now written beside it: anything that ever lets the section count past 65535 must replace that spread with a reduce. This is the `bc9fc9f` shape (a `RangeError` blanking a whole tab) one step from being reachable.
  - **Fifteen negative controls, fourteen discriminating first time, and THE ONE INERT CONTROL IS RECORDED RATHER THAN QUIETLY REPAIRED.** Each bound was removed or inverted and the row confirmed red: the debug count cap and section limit (1 red each), the export count cap, both relocation bounds, `MAX_DER_CHILDREN`, the unwind budget, `MAX_PDATA_ENTRIES`, the string budget, the import desync **restored verbatim**, all three import flag paths, and `nygv`'s own `MAX_PDB_PATH_BYTES` (3 red, i.e. this change did not weaken it). **The inert one was the export section bound**, and the inertness was a fault in the *test*: its fixture used a 0x220-byte section in a ~0x1000-byte file and asserted that no name bound, so with the bound removed the extra slots were file **padding**, which reads as RVA 0 and is unmapped — the row stayed green over the defect. The fixture now puts 128 resolvable name pointers in a *different section*, so removing the bound binds names the export directory never listed. A bound whose control cannot be made red is a bound with no test.
  - **Every reader is unchanged on well-formed input, and for a parser change that is the case rather than a formality** — a cap that truncates a real file has traded a hang for a wrong answer, which is the worse of the two. `npm run corpus:parserdiff` is **byte-identical** to its 98-of-118-green baseline (20 vacuous, 0 red) over all six real binaries, including every relocation block and entry, all 92 IAT slots and function names, imphash and 20 header fields; `npm run corpus` is byte-identical over all four x86 binaries (260/279/258/275 functions, 3317 guards, `VERDICT: no regression`); `npm run corpus:arm64` is 51 of 51 green. Each group in `malformed.test.ts` additionally carries its own well-formed safety row.
  - **Two rows in this family were REFUSED and handed back, both `catch {}` blocks in `parsePE` that render something FALSE.** `parseSecurityDirectory`'s makes a malformed certificate render `HeaderView`'s grey **"Unsigned"** pill and "No digital signature found in this binary", and `parseResourceDirectory`'s renders "No resources found in this PE file" — in both cases "we could not read it" is reported as "there is none", which is exactly the distinction this codebase insists on everywhere else. They are refused here rather than fixed because **the render site is the whole of the fix** and both render sites (`HeaderView.tsx`, `ResourcesView.tsx`, plus `resources.ts` for the second) are outside this change's ownership; smuggling a new `PEFile` channel plus two component changes into a bounds commit would make neither reviewable. `ResourceTree.truncated`'s own half-built state — the flag has been set since before `tmo9` and **no view has ever rendered it** — is the standing precedent for why that is worth its own change. `parseDebugDirectory` has the same shape at smaller stakes: a record whose *path* was cut short says so in the value, but a directory whose *entry list* was cut short says nothing, because the return type is a bare `DebugInfo[]` and `HeaderView` would have to change to carry a flag. **Both were taken up in the next change and are the entry below.**

- **A DISTINGUISHED NAME IS NOT ITS CN, AND A PKCS#7 `certificates` SET IS NOT ONE CERTIFICATE.** Two narrowings in `pe/authenticode.ts` that no type, flag or view recorded (`peek-a-bin-4q8w`, filed out of `peek-a-bin-8pod`'s hand-back). `extractCN` walked the RDNSequence for the commonName and dropped O, OU, L, ST, C and everything else, while the panel's row is labelled **"Subject"** — which in X.509 *is* the DN. So two publishers sharing a CN and differing in O were one string on the page, and the half of the DN that distinguishes least was the half that survived, which is precisely backwards for an analyst comparing a signature against a known-good one. Separately, `parsePKCS7` took `certs[0]` and reported no count, so a signature carrying the leaf plus intermediates — i.e. every real one — described one certificate as though there were one.
  - **`subject`/`issuer` now hold the whole DN and `subjectCN`/`issuerCN` the CN alone**, rather than adding a second row. The row's label already claimed the DN; a caller that wants the short form has it without parsing the string back apart. Rendered in **encoding order, not RFC 2253's reversed order**: the point of the string is to be compared with the file and with what another tool printed from the same bytes, and reversing it would make the two disagree for no gain.
  - **An attribute type this parser has no short name for is spelled as a DOTTED OID, not skipped** — skipping is what made a DN read as its CN. `decodeOID` is ten lines (first byte packs two arcs, the rest base-128) and **refuses a truncated or unterminated encoding rather than returning a partial OID**, since a partial OID names a *different* attribute type. Both halves are controls: skip unknown attributes → 1 red, accept an unterminated arc → 1 red.
  - **`BMPString` and `T61String` are decoded BY HAND.** `TextDecoder("utf-16be")` and `"t61"` are label lookups a runtime built without full ICU may not have, and a decoder that throws on a signature field is a worse outcome than a few lines of shifting. Microsoft's own timestamp certificates carry BMPStrings, so this is not a hypothetical. `UniversalString` is deliberately **not** decoded and its attribute is skipped. `readDERString`'s `tag` parameter is optional and only ever widens, so every pre-existing caller (the two time formats) is unchanged.
  - **`certificateCount` is a COUNT, and the name says so because the SET is not a validated chain**: it is unordered and may hold intermediates, cross-certificates or certificates unrelated to the signer. `undefined` (the walk never reached the SET), `0` (empty) and `1` are three different facts; the panel shows the row only **above 1**, since "1" tells a reader nothing and a row on every signed file is noise. Its control is the row appearing at 1.
  - **Nine negative controls, all discriminating.** DN back to the CN → 6 red (four of them pre-existing rows); unknown OIDs skipped → 1; only the first RDN read → 3; `certificateCount` never set → 4; the panel's row dropped → 1; the panel's row always shown → 1; BMPString read as UTF-8 → 1; T61String read as UTF-8 → 1; an unterminated OID accepted → 1.
  - **THE FIXTURES HAD TO LAND FIRST, which is why this was filed rather than done in passing.** Both `pe/__tests__/fixtures.ts`' `buildPKCS7` and `authenticode.test.ts`' own local builder emitted one certificate with a CN-only DN, so there was nothing to fail against — the `peek-a-bin-6qx9` pattern. They now take `subjectAttrs`/`issuerAttrs` and `certificateCount`, and the extra certificates carry a distinguishing CN so a reader taking the wrong element of the SET shows up on the page.
  - **The evidence is a fixture and nothing else, and that is the honest statement of it.** No binary on this machine is signed — all six corpus images declare no certificate table at all — so `corpus:parserdiff` has nothing to say here, and no human has seen a signed image in the browser (`peek-a-bin-v2u`). `npm run corpus` byte-identical.

- **…AND THE ONE NARROWING ORDINARY INPUT REACHES HAD NO CHANNEL AT ALL: the string scan's.** Every other admission in `src/pe/` needs a crafted or absurd file. This one needs only a section with more than `SECTION_SCAN_LIMIT` (1 MiB) of raw data in it — and `extractStrings` returned `{ strings, stringTypes }`, so the Strings tab's count, `pe://{id}/strings` and the exported report's `| Strings | N |` row all stated a clipped list's length as a fact about the file (`peek-a-bin-2py5`, handed back by `peek-a-bin-8pod` because the channel had to cross a worker RPC and `AppState`).
  - **`scanEndFor` IS THE ONE PLACE BOTH BOUNDS APPLY, so the fact is collected there** — `SECTION_SCAN_LIMIT` per section and `MAX_STRING_SCAN_BYTES` (64 MiB) for the whole call, shared by the four passes. `ScanBudget.reach` is keyed on the `SectionHeader` **object**, not its name: a crafted image may declare several sections called `.rdata` and two of them are two populations of bytes.
  - **THE RECORDED REACH IS THE MAXIMUM ACROSS PASSES, and that is a real choice with a discriminating control.** Three passes read each data section (ASCII, UTF-16, pointer). By the time the third reaches an early section the global budget may be spent, so *the last pass's* answer would report a section the first pass read completely: measured on 400 overlapping sections, `max` reports **97** clipped and the last-pass reading reports ~400. Summing treble-counts and reddens the per-section row instead. Both perturbations are controls.
  - **BYTES THE FILE DOES NOT HOLD ARE NOT REPORTED.** `available` is clamped to the buffer, so a section header describing more raw data than the file contains — a carved sample, a part-finished download — is *not* an unscanned one. Folding the two would put "bytes unscanned" on every truncated image, over bytes that were never there to read. Its own control.
  - **A LIST CANNOT CARRY THE MARKER, so this is a flag plus the counts** — `TRUNCATION_MARKER` works for a *value*, and an invented entry would be a falsehood inside a Map that feeds the xref maps and the disassembly's inline comments (`peek-a-bin-tmo9`'s argument for `importsTruncated`). `PEFile.stringScan` is the field, absent when the scan covered everything, and it is **spread rather than assigned** through `SET_STRINGS` so a complete scan cannot write `undefined` over it. `parseAdmissions` gains a sixth subject whose sentence says the row is reached by ordinary binaries — the only one of the six that can say so.
  - **The channel is four hops and each one is separately controlled**: `extractStrings` → the `extractStrings` RPC reply → `disasmClient`'s unpacker (where the strings are rebuilt into Maps and a third field is exactly what an unpacker drops) → `SET_STRINGS` → `PEFile`. `mcp/session.ts` assigns it onto its own `pe` instead, which is what `parseAdmissions` reads for `load_pe` and the strings resource.
  - **Ten negative controls, all discriminating.** Coverage never reported → 2 red; `available` unclamped → 1; reach = last pass → 1; reach = sum → 1; the view's notice dropped → 1; the view's notice always shown → 1; the reducer dropping the field → 1; the reducer writing it unconditionally → 1; the dispatch reply dropping it → 2; the client's unpacker dropping it → 1.
  - **HOW MUCH IS MEASURED, since it is the reason this row was raised above its siblings.** The bound is in the code; that a real image reaches it is INFERRED from `MAX_STRING_SCAN_BYTES`' own docstring ("a few MiB" of `.rdata`/`.data` on a 253 MiB image), quoted rather than re-measured. **The largest raw section across the six corpus binaries is 112,640 bytes** (`t64-arm.exe`, at `12ff998`), an order of magnitude under the cap, so all six report nothing, `parseAdmissions` returns `[]` for every one, and the liveness half is necessarily a fixture. `npm run corpus` byte-identical, `corpus:parserdiff` 98/118 with 0 red.

- **AN ADMISSION THAT STOPS AT THE BROWSER UI IS NO ADMISSION AT ALL ON THE TWO SURFACES THAT OUTLIVE THE SESSION.** Measured by grep at `d8d8a6d`: **no module under `src/mcp/` read any truncation flag** — not `importsTruncated`, not `ImportEntry.truncated`, not `ResourceTree.truncated` — so `pe://{id}/imports` handed a client a short list as a complete one; and `utils/exportSchema.ts` reproduced the same short list in a report while **introducing a truncation of its own**, `str.slice(0, 60) + "..."`, spelled differently from every other truncation in the tool. Both outlive the session that produced them: an MCP answer is consumed by something that **cannot ask a follow-up question**, and an exported report is a file the user keeps and may compare against another tool months later (`peek-a-bin-8pod`, the last unfiled row of `peek-a-bin-ul9m`'s census).
  - **THE VALUE IS A SENTENCE, NOT A FLAG, AND THAT IS THE DESIGN.** `pe/admissions.ts`'s `parseAdmissions(pe)` returns `{ subject, sentence }` rows. The consumers are an **LLM** and a **human reading a file later**; neither can be relied on to know what `truncated: true` means, and an LLM in particular has no way at all to notice that a count looks small. So the prose carries the fact on `TRUNCATION_MARKER`'s model — a consumer that has never heard of the field cannot be fooled by the value — while `subject` is the closed union a consumer that *has* heard of it keys off. **Empty means the parse was whole**, so `length` is the test; no "everything is fine" row is ever emitted. The sentences are asserted to contain no `|` and no newline, because both consumers embed them in a formatted document.
  - **Why it is not `analysis/anomalies.ts`, which reports two of the same facts.** That pass answers *what about this file should an analyst look at*, carries a severity, and includes findings with nothing to do with the parse (WX sections, entropy, an entry point in writable memory). This answers *how much of what you are reading is actually there*, and its consumers are formats rather than a screen. The overlap is two **sentences**, not a predicate: both read `certificateUnreadable` / `resourcesUnreadable`, which are the single declaration either way.
  - **The MCP list resources moved the array under a key, and the wrapper is UNCONDITIONAL.** `pe://{id}/imports` is `{ incomplete?, libraries: [...] }` and `pe://{id}/exports` is `{ incomplete?, exports: [...] }` — a cut-short table is shaped exactly like a complete small one, so a bare array has nowhere to say it is short. Unconditional because a shape that changes with the file is worse to consume than one extra key; `incomplete` itself is **absent rather than false**, on `PEFile.importsTruncated`'s own rule. `ImportEntry.truncated` is carried **per library** beside it, because each descriptor has its own thunk walk — one library's list can be short while the rest are whole, and the whole-table fact is the one with no row to hang on. `load_pe`'s summary gained `incomplete?: string[]`, since its counts are the first and often only thing a client learns about a file.
  - **The report says it twice, in two places, on purpose.** A blockquote directly under the Summary table (that is what it qualifies — a reader takes those counts as facts otherwise), and a note on the `## Imports` / `## Exports` headings plus each `### <library>`, because a reader who has scrolled to the list has left the table behind. The test for the two is **split into two `it()`s deliberately**: within one, the first failure hides the second, so a control withdrawing only the heading notes would look identical to one withdrawing the whole block.
  - **The report's own clip now uses `TRUNCATION_MARKER`.** Three dots cannot be told from a string that legitimately ends in them, and `isTruncatedValue` cannot recognise them. **A guard for "the parser already marked this" was considered and left out as INERT**: `extractStrings` marks nothing — its budget cuts the *scan*, not a string — so nothing in `pe.strings` can arrive marked, and an inert guard reads as a rule that holds.
  - **Eight negative controls, every one discriminating.** `parseAdmissions` returning nothing → 12 red; reporting imports unconditionally → 6 red (three of them the whole-parse controls, which is the half that catches a fix marking everything); the report's block withdrawn → 1; its heading notes withdrawn → 1 (a *different* row, after the split); the bare-ellipsis clip restored → 1; the MCP `incomplete` key dropped → 3; the per-library note dropped → 1; `load_pe`'s list dropped → 1.
  - **`generateMarkdownReport` had NO test at all before this**, which is how a report reproducing a narrowed parse as a complete one went unnoticed. `npm run corpus` is byte-identical over the four binaries (`parsePE` is untouched by this half), `--dir src` 4848 in 142 files.
  - **Still not carried, and named rather than implied**: `extractStrings`' `MAX_STRING_SCAN_BYTES` budget has no channel anywhere (a flag would have to cross a worker RPC and `AppState`); `DebugDirectory.truncated` and `DebugInfo.pdbPathTruncated` reach neither consumer, that reader being called by the one panel that renders it; the 256-callback TLS cap; and every `authenticode.ts` narrowing (a certificate *chain* read as one certificate, a Distinguished Name read as a CN). `ExportSchemaV1` needs nothing: it carries annotations, not parser-derived lists.

- **The truncation marker is ONE declaration now, and the reason it was worth a change of its own is that the marker is READ BACK.** `TRUNCATION_MARKER` + `isTruncatedValue` in `pe/truncation.ts` replace `PDB_PATH_TRUNCATION_MARKER` (`metadata.ts`, `nygv`), `NAME_TRUNCATION_MARKER` + `isNameTruncated` (`parser.ts`, `tmo9`) and `RESOURCE_STRING_TRUNCATION_MARKER` (`resources.ts`, `dhcx`) — three identical literals, each landed by a change that did not own the other two files, each with a docstring admitting the duplication, and the last one filing the consolidation (`peek-a-bin-wo8g`, out of `peek-a-bin-8pod`'s note).
  - **Why this is not the ordinary "three copies of a constant" case.** `isTruncatedValue` is what decides `ImportEntry.truncated`, which is what makes `computeImphash` refuse; and `utils/exportSchema.ts` now recognises a marked value in a string it did not produce. So a drifted copy would not merely look different on screen — a **reader would silently stop recognising the admission**, which is the `Ordinal_<n>` wire-format trap in a second guise.
  - **The literal moved; the per-reader REASONING did not.** Each site's asymmetry is a real decision and stays where the append happens: `readCString` marks the value **and** the entry, because these names reach a digest, `matchesApi`, `resolveOrdinal` and the IAT map; `readResourceString` and `readWString` mark the value alone, because nothing in `resources.ts` feeds a digest; the PDB path likewise. Folding those docstrings into the constant would have made one shared paragraph out of three different judgements.
  - **The drift guard is a text scrape and is written like this repo's others.** `src/pe/__tests__/truncation.test.ts` asserts exactly one file under `src/` contains the literal, matched as **the marker's own characters** rather than as a `const` line — so a reformat, a rename or a change of quoting cannot slip a copy past it — with the module-under-test's presence in the walk as the **liveness half**, since a scrape fails by matching nothing. Plus the marker's *property* (characters no name, path or resource string these readers return can contain) and both directions of the predicate, including that a value merely *containing* the marker is not truncated: a crafted name must not be able to claim the admission for itself. Control: reintroduce a copy in `metadata.ts` → 1 red.

- **A DIRECTORY THE FILE DECLARES AND THE READER GAVE UP ON IS NOT A DIRECTORY THE FILE LACKS — and the channel for saying so is DERIVED, not a new `PEFile` flag.** The two rows the bounds sweep refused above, plus `parseDebugDirectory`'s entry list, which is the same shape at smaller stakes. `parsePE` reads the certificate table and the resource directory inside a `catch {}`; each leaves its field `undefined`, which is byte-for-byte what a file that genuinely has neither produces — so `HeaderView` rendered the grey **"Unsigned"** pill over an image whose optional header declares an attribute certificate, and `ResourcesView` rendered "No resources found in this PE file." over a declared resource directory. Both are positive claims about the FILE resting on the tool's own failure to read it, i.e. the one distinction this codebase insists on everywhere else (`computeImphash`'s `null`, `PDB_PATH_TRUNCATION_MARKER`, `ResourceTree.truncated`, `DetectResult.omitted`) erased in the two places nothing was watching (`peek-a-bin-wo8g`).
  - **`peek-a-bin-dd94`'s CRITERION IS WHAT DECIDES THE CHANNEL, and applied here it says: derive.** The question is *does the output already carry the fact?* For a truncated import list it did not — a list cut short at a bound is shaped exactly like a complete short one — so `importsTruncated` was right. For the data-directory clamp it did (`dataDirectories.length < numberOfRvaAndSizes` **is** the clamp), so a flag was refused as a second declaration that can drift. This case is the second kind and the bead's own guess was the first: `parsePE` opens each of these readers behind exactly "the directory is declared", so **"declared, and absent from the parse" is the failure**, over two fields that are already public. `directoryDeclared`, `certificateUnreadable` and `resourcesUnreadable` (`pe/dataDirectories.ts`, beside `dataDirectoryClamp` for the same reason) are the one declaration, and `parser.ts`'s resource gate is now the *same call* — which is the load-bearing part, because a view whose premise drifts from the parser's gate claims a read failed on a directory nothing tried to read.
  - **`parseSecurityDirectory` REACHES THIS WITHOUT THROWING, which is why the certificate half is a repair and not a guard.** It answers `null` for a declared directory in exactly one circumstance — `fileOffset + 8 > buffer.byteLength`, the `WIN_CERTIFICATE` header not fitting in the file, i.e. a truncated download or a carved sample — and for every *other* malformation it returns `signed: true` with null fields, which the panel already renders honestly (`dwLength` overrunning, a non-PKCS#7 type, a DER walk that finds nothing). So the derived pair covers the `catch` and this return with one predicate, and the fixture is a real parse: `dataDirectories: new Map([[4, { virtualAddress: 0x100000, size: 0x200 }]])` → `certificate` undefined, directory declared, pill **Unreadable**.
  - **THE RESOURCE HALF'S POPULATION IS EMPTY AND THAT IS STATED AT THREE SITES RATHER THAN IMPLIED.** `parseResourceDirectory` bounds every read on the buffer, recurses to a fixed `MAX_DEPTH`, and answers a `truncated` tree for an RVA that resolves nowhere (`dhcx`), so **nothing on this machine can make it throw** — the arm is a guard on a `catch` that is currently unreachable, and its test builds the state directly and says so. Landing it anyway is the cheap half of the trade: the day a reader does throw, the pane otherwise says the file has no resources.
  - **The truncated tree must NOT be reported unreadable, and that is the row that keeps the two admissions apart.** `dhcx`'s sentence ("could not be read whole", naming `MAX_TOTAL_ENTRIES`) is strictly more informative than this one, and a predicate reading `!pe.resources?.entries.length` instead of `pe.resources === undefined` would make it unreachable. The arms are disjoint by construction — `truncated` is a field on a tree that exists — so their **order is inert and is not claimed to be load-bearing**.
  - **`parseDebugDirectory` now returns `DebugDirectory`, and widening the return type is what forced the render site to be revisited** — `computeImphash`'s widened-parameter mechanism, one level down. `IMAGE_DEBUG_DIRECTORY` has no count field: the count *is* `size / 28`, so `numEntries < floor(size / 28)` is **exact**, which is why the flag can be derived once at the end rather than set at each of the three bounds (`MAX_DEBUG_DIRECTORY_ENTRIES`, the section's raw extent, the buffer). The unmapped-RVA early return sets it too, for `dhcx`'s reason. **A list cannot carry `nygv`'s marker** — an invented row would be a falsehood inside the data — so the admission is the type plus the table's heading, and the block now renders on `truncated` as well as on a non-empty list: a declared directory whose RVA resolves nowhere read zero entries and made the whole block vanish, which is the same silence as the grey pill.
  - **The anomalies pass gets both findings, for `dd94`'s reason and not by symmetry.** Each panel marks the fact where a reader looking at that directory would see it; `analysis/anomalies.ts` is the surface an analyst opens on purpose, and someone who never opens the Resources tab never learns the file declares resources nothing could walk. `warning`, on the clamp's argument: the file is malformed and no toolchain produces either by accident, but nothing here says code will run.
  - **Seven negative controls, every one discriminating, none inert.** Revert the signature block to two states → 1 red; make `certificateUnreadable` skip `directoryDeclared` → **6 red**, four of them pre-existing rows (every unsigned fixture in the suite starts claiming "Unreadable"); the same for `resourcesUnreadable` → **4 red**, two pre-existing; `truncated = false` in `parseDebugDirectory` → 4 red; `truncated = true` → 3 red, the well-formed-directory rows; the debug block back to `entries.length > 0` → 1 red; the unmapped-RVA return back to a bare empty list → 1 red.
  - **No false positive on any real binary, which is the half a green suite cannot show.** All six corpus binaries: `certificateUnreadable` false and `resourcesUnreadable` false on every one, and every debug directory reads exactly the entries it declares (1 on the four x86 images, 3 on the two ARM64 ones — `size` 28 and 84, unchanged from `d8d8a6d`). All six declare resources and **none declares a certificate**, so "Unsigned" is what every real file here still says. `npm run corpus` byte-identical over the four x86 binaries (emitted C identical for all 275 functions), `corpus:parserdiff` 98/118 with 20 vacuous and 0 red, `corpus:arm64` 51/51.
  - **Left undone deliberately**: the admissions still do not reach MCP output or the export file (`peek-a-bin-8pod`, the widest remaining row of the `ul9m` census — an LLM consumer cannot notice that a count looks small), and no human has seen either sentence in a browser (`peek-a-bin-v2u`).

- **`regSize()` is not a membership test.** It falls back to `4` for any unrecognised name, so `regSize(x) > 0` is true for every string. Use `isKnownRegister()` (`decompile/ir.ts`). This exact mistake made `lifter.ts`'s `isRegister()` a no-op that lifted immediates as registers.
- **`RegState.defs` is keyed by the literal operand text, and that is deliberate — ask `wroteAnyAlias` instead of canonicalising the map.** The map stores the *expression* last written, and that expression has the operand's width, so a key of `rcx` would record `mov cl, 2`'s one byte as if it were eight; on x86-64 a byte write does not even clear the upper bits a 32-bit write does. But *arity* is a width-blind question — `mov ecx, 1 / call f` passes an argument in RCX exactly as `mov rcx, 1` does — and `collectArgs64` probed the literal 64-bit name, missed every sub-width setup, and broke out of the loop there. With no argument in the IR the write had no reader and DCE deleted it: `ExitProcess()` with the exit code gone, in valid well-typed C, 5 times in each x64 binary. `wroteAnyAlias` answers the width-blind question over the width-exact map (same shape as `invalidateCallerSaved`), and returns a boolean so the recorded expression can never be substituted at the call site — `peek-a-bin-urs` cannot come back through it. **The suite had pinned the defect as the rule** (`it("misses x64 arguments set up through 32-bit sub-registers")`, asserting `[]` under a KNOWN BUG comment), which is why nothing failed for as long as it stood. Measured: 156/279 t64 and 154/275 w64 functions changed emitted text, t32/w32 byte-identical (`collectArgs32` reads pushes, not `RegState`), and against `apitypes.ts`'s declared signatures — the only oracle here that can see arity, since `gcc -std=gnu89` accepts an implicit declaration at any arity — exact-arity calls went 64→90 and 70→96 with over-count unmoved at 3 (`peek-a-bin-qb2x`). **Those four figures are stamped at the qb2x measurement and detection has moved since; the audit is now `corpus/arity.ts` and HEAD's numbers are in the Verification section.** The instrument that produced 64→90 was lost with a scratch worktree and the claim was unrepeatable for a while (`peek-a-bin-02fa`) — when you build an oracle to verify a change, land the oracle.
- **A written fastcall register is not an argument if the block already SPENT it as an address index — and base and index are different evidence.** `collectArgs64` asks `RegState` only *whether* the block wrote RCX/RDX/R8/R9, which is equally true of a register the block computed for its own addressing: t64 0x14000B34B does `imul rcx, rcx, 0x58` / `and BYTE PTR [rax+rcx*1+8], 0xfe` / `call GetLastError`, and the emitted C passed an argument to an API declaring none. The discriminator is that the value has been **spent as an index**: a subscript, scaled by an element size, whose access has already been emitted. An address **base** proves nothing and must not be treated the same — `lea rdx, [rcx+0x10]` / `f(rcx, rdx)` is a pointer that *is* the argument. `liftBlock` notes index reads via `noteIndexReads` before dispatching each instruction, so a read-modify-write clears its own mark when it calls `regState.set`; `RegState.consumed` holds them and `invalidateCallerSaved` drops the volatile ones. **One exemption, and it is the prefix property rather than a patch:** an index read does not spend the register when the instruction's destination is a fastcall register *later* in the argument order — `mov rdx, QWORD PTR [rdx+rcx*8]` is argument two derived from argument one, and since arity is counted as a prefix, RDX being an argument already implies RCX is. Without it t64!sub_14000FCE7 emitted `sub_14000278C()` for a callee that reads both ECX and RDX, and the statements computing them were then deleted as dead — `peek-a-bin-qb2x`'s failure mode. **Four rules refuted by this same corpus, none of which may be re-tried:** *distance* and *dominance* from the write to the call (the filed hypothesis, refuted by every one of the six rows — `RegState` is per-block so the write always dominates, and it is two instructions from the call at 0x14000B35F and 0x14000369C); *any* read spends it (t64 0x14000FAF0 `mov DWORD PTR [rsp+0x20], r8d` spills R8 to the outgoing stack-argument area **because** it is also the register argument, and `CreateFileW` loses two of four); and any read from inside a memory operand spends it (t64 0x14000BD6E `lea edx, [r9+0x8]` is MSVC computing the constant 9 from the 1 it just put in R9 — arithmetic wearing an address's clothes — and R9 is argument four of the `MultiByteToWideChar` two instructions later). Measured on base `7082e66`: arity over **3→0 on both x64 binaries**, exact 90→93/127 and 96→99/133, **under and both ceiling splits unmoved at 34 (26/8)**, x86 emitted C byte-identical, four functions changed per x64 binary, and every other audit — gcc, offsetof, polarity, loop exits, callees lost, statement drops, unrecovered values, stale reads, stale guards — unmoved. **The best evidence is again a callee `apitypes.ts` cannot see**: t64's `sub_140007808(expr, rdx)` and `sub_140007908(expr, rdx)` became one-argument calls, and both callees begin `movsxd r, ecx` and never read RDX. **Never answer this question from `apitypes.ts`** — same reason as below. `qb2x` is untouched: `wroteAnyAlias` still answers width-blind, and the 5 `ExitProcess` and 4 `Sleep` calls per x64 binary keep their arguments (`peek-a-bin-7r1l`).
- **`collectArgs32`'s backwards push-walk stops dead at a call whose result feeds a *following* call, and the marker is AFTER the call, not before it.** The shape is `call inner` / `push eax` / `call outer`: the inner call's result is pushed, so the inner call is an argument expression of the outer one and the pushes *above* it are the outer call's. Verified on t32.exe at 0x40e08b (`push ebx` / `push 0x8` / `call GetProcessHeap` / `push eax` / `call HeapAlloc`) and 0x402c4a (`push esi` / `call GetCurrentProcess` / `push eax` / `call TerminateProcess`) — **there is no call *between* the pushes and the inner call**, which is why the tempting rule "stop the walk at an intervening call boundary" would never fire once. 19 such marker sites on t32 and 18 on w32; ~7 and ~6 emitted calls actually lose arguments, the rest having had none to steal. It is deliberately an **admitted under-count, not a re-attribution**: handing the pushes to the outer call instead is a guess in the *over*-count direction, and an invented argument is the one error this codebase will not trade for a recovered one. In this corpus every callee the rule fires on takes zero arguments, so all of them land on `exact` — arity over went **8→4 on t32 and 10→6 on w32, exact 70→74/105 and 73→77/111**, x64 byte-identical (`collectArgs32` is the x86 path only). **The best evidence is a callee `apitypes.ts` cannot see**: t32's `sub_402283(edi, esi)` became `sub_402283()`, and that function is called with no preceding pushes at 10 of its 13 call sites, so it provably takes none — the two arguments were invented and no oracle here would ever have reported it. Costs, both benign and both measured: two struct shapes on each x86 binary stop merging (`distinctDefs` 57→59 and 56→58, per-preamble `fields` 358→348 and 413→403, **every layout still correct in both runs**), which renumbers `struct_N` across ~43 t32 and ~41 w32 functions whose text is otherwise unchanged (`peek-a-bin-f51x`).
- **A `push` of a callee-saved register the function has not yet written is a register SAVE, and that — not the register, not the position — is the whole discriminator.** `collectArgs32`'s backwards walk had no way to stop before the prologue, so it emitted `GetModuleHandleW("KERNEL32.DLL", edi)` for an API declaring one parameter and `GetCommandLineW(edi, esi, ebx)` for one declaring none. **Two tempting rules are refuted by this same corpus and neither may be re-tried.** "A push of ebx/esi/edi is a save" dies at t32 0x402c3f, where `push esi` *is* an argument. "A save is identifiable by position plus a matching `pop` before the `ret`" dies twice over: `push imm8 / pop reg` is a pervasive MSVC size idiom, so in t32's `sub_401A4E` — itself an over-counting site — *every* `pop ebx`/`pop edi` in the function is `mov reg, imm`; and the saves are often not at the entry, sitting six instructions in behind the allocation and the GS cookie at 0x401a4e, or *sunk* to a mid-function block leader reached by `jne` at 0x4086f1. What separates them is whether the pushed value is the register's **entry** value, decided at t32 0x402c35 where the same register in the same block is both — `push esi` (save) / `mov esi, 0xc0000417` / `push esi` (argument). `firstCalleeSavedWrites` (`lifter.ts`) precomputes the lowest address at which each of ebx/esi/edi/ebp is written and `decompileFunction` threads it through `liftBlock`; a push below its register's first write ends the walk. Three constraints are load-bearing: the scope is **function-wide** (w32 0x40104d is a genuine `push esi` argument at a block *leader*, with esi written in an earlier block), **`mov X, X` is not a definition** but `xor X, X` very much is (the hot-patch pad is the entry instruction of two of the four t32 rows; generalising the self-move test past `mov` withdrew t32 0x4068c6's zeroing and turned four real `Sleep(esi)` calls per x86 binary into `Sleep()`), and the rule is **restricted to the four callee-saved registers** — on cdecl and stdcall every incoming argument arrives on the stack, so their entry values are opaque to the callee and forwarding one is meaningless, which is what makes the rule sound rather than empirical on ten rows. The address-order approximation is deliberate and one-directional: a write laid out after a push that dynamically precedes it drops an argument, never invents one. Measured on base `91cca4f`: arity over **4→0 on t32 and 6→0 on w32**, exact 75→79/105 and 79→85/111, **under unmoved at 26 on both**, x64 emitted C byte-identical, and every other audit — gcc, offsetof fields *and* distinct definitions, polarity, loop exits, callees lost, statement drops, unrecovered values, stale reads — unmoved (`peek-a-bin-6lmh`). **Never answer this question from `apitypes.ts`.** It would drive over to 0 for exactly the callees `corpus/arity.ts` can see, making the audit measure its own input and blinding the only oracle here that can see call arity at all.
- **The CSP is generated, not hand-written.** Edit `build/csp.ts`, never `nginx.conf`'s header or `index.html` directly — `build/csp.test.ts` fails on drift. A meta CSP cannot be committed into `index.html` because it is also the dev entry point and Vite injects an inline React Refresh preamble there. Note the shipped `connect-src` omits non-localhost plain `http:`, so a LAN Ghidra server is blocked on the HTTP nginx deployment.
- **Do not re-add a plugin that copies `capstone.wasm`.** Rollup already rewrites `new URL("capstone.wasm", import.meta.url)` to its hashed asset; a manual copy is pure duplication and was 1.7 MiB of the PWA precache. `capstone-wasm-guard` in `vite.config.ts` fails the build if more than one WASM asset is emitted.
- **`tools.ts` and `resources.ts` must only *type*-import `./session`.** A value import pulls in `./disasm`, which loads Capstone WASM at module scope, and both MCP suites become slow and fragile. `src/mcp/__tests__/importGraph.test.ts` enforces this.
- **`biome.json` must be strict JSON.** A single `//` comment silently voids the whole config and Biome falls back to defaults — which looks like your rule settings randomly stopped applying. `build/lintConfig.test.ts` fails on a comment and on `useExhaustiveDependencies` dropping below `error`; the seven a11y rules are at `error` too (301 findings were cleared), so keep all of it there.
